the requirements for the degree of

Doctor of Philosophy

University of Washington - Seattle

2024

Theoretical Mathematics

University of Washington

Abstract

Towards Cohomology of Real Closed Spaces

Chair of Supervisory Committee:

Sándor Kovács

Theoretical Mathematics

It was shown by Claus Scheiderer prior to 1994 that real closed spaces have étale cohomology. Following Scheiderer, study of real closed spaces fell out of fashion and o-minimal geometry became the focus for those at the intersection of model theory and geometry. I decided to breathe new life into the theory of real closed rings and spaces, as studied by Schwartz in 1989. In Section 1, I build the fundamentals of the theory using as little machinery as possible, and presented them as clearly as I could. Hidden gems include a full proof that real closed rings are closed under limits and colimits. In Section 2, I give an introduction to the category of real closed spaces in the first half. In the second half, I construct an equivalence of topoi between Scheiderer’s sheaves on the real étale site, and sheaves on a real étale site
\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X
of my creation. Since
\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X)
can be defined without the use of
G
-topoi, the equivalence of topoi renders Scheiderer’s theory computable. I end with a discussion of how one might use motivic cohomology to better understand recent results of Annette Huber in
[
3
]
.

1.
Sper(-) and Sections

1.1.
Orders=Cones

The Goal of this section is to introduce the ideas necessary to define
\text{Sper}(R)
, for
R
a commutative ring with
1\in R
, as a topological space with both the weak (read: Zariski) topology and the pro-constructible topology, together with sheaves of compatible and constructible sections. We assume some background in commutative algebra and point set topology, though quite minimal amounts of each.

Let
R
be a commutative ring with
1\in R
.

Definition 1
.

A
positive cone of a partial order

\alpha
on
R
is a subset
\alpha\subset R
such that

(1)

0\in\alpha
,

(2)

+_{R}:\alpha\times\alpha\to\alpha
is a function,

(3)

*_{R}:\alpha\times\alpha\to\alpha
is a function, and

(4)

\forall x\in\alpha
, either
x=0
or
-x\notin\alpha
.

\alpha
is the
positive cone of a total order
if we additionally require that

\forall x\in R,x=0\bigvee\left((x\in\alpha)\bigwedge(-x\notin\alpha)\right)\bigvee\left((-x\in\alpha)\bigwedge(x\notin\alpha)\right).

We say
\alpha\subset R

partially orders

R
if
\alpha
is the positive cone of a partial order on
R
, and we say
\alpha

totally orders

R
if
\alpha
is the positive cone of a total order on
R
.

We observe that if a subset
\alpha
of a ring
R
is the positive cone of a total order on
R
, then
\alpha
satisfies conditions (1)-(3), meaning
\alpha
is a commutative semiring and a sub-semiring of
R
. An example of a positive cone of a total order is

\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}:x\geq 0\}

for the commutative ring
\mathbb{Z}
.

Reflecting further on
\mathbb{Z}
with positive cone (short for positive cone of total order)
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we define a binary relation
\prec
on
\mathbb{Z}
as follows; for
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}

x\prec y\qquad\iff\qquad y-x\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

If
x=y
,
y-x=0
, so by (1) in our definition of positive cones,
x\prec x
. If
x\prec y
and
y\neq x
, then
y-x\in\alpha=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and consequently

-(y-x)=x-y\notin\alpha

and
\prec
is antisymmetric. Finally, if
x\prec y
and
y\prec z
,

y-x,z-y\in\alpha=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

so by property (2),

(z-y)+(y-x)=z-x\in\alpha=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

implying
x\prec z
and that
\prec
is transitive.

Thus a positive cone of a total order
\alpha\subset R
does give a total order
\prec
on the ring
R
. On the other hand, given a ring
R
with total order
\prec
,

\{x\in R:0\prec x\}

is closed under addition and multiplication by properties of ordered rings, contains 0, and contains no pair
\{y,-y\}
for
y\in R
, so it
is
the positive cone of its total order.

We need two more definition which will be handy later;

Definition 2
.

(
[
5
]
,1) Let
R
be a commutative unital ring with positive cone of total order
\alpha
. An ideal
I\leq R
is
\alpha
-
convex
if the following sentence holds for all
a,b\in R
;

If
a,b-a\in\alpha
, and
b\in I
, then
a\in I
.

The definition above explains how we can define a notion of convexity in an arbitrary commutative ring.

Definition 3
.

(
[
8
]
,2) Let
R
be a commutative unital ring, with positive cone of partial order
\alpha\subset R
. The
support
of
\alpha
is the set

\supp(\alpha):=\alpha\cap-\alpha

The support
\supp(\alpha)
of a positive cone of partial order
\alpha\subset R
is closed under addition; for
r_{1},r_{2}\in\supp(\alpha)
, we have
r_{i}=-s_{i}
for some
s_{i}\in\alpha
, and consequently

-s_{1}+-s_{2}=-(s_{1}+s_{2})\in-\alpha.

\supp(\alpha)
is similarly closed under multiplication because
(-1)^{2}=1
, and
0\in\supp(\alpha)
.

Theorem 4
.

If
R
is a ring with positive cone of partial order
\alpha
, then
\supp(\alpha)
is an ideal in
R
.

Proof.

Consider the function

*_{R}:R\times\supp(\alpha)\to R.

We may decompose
R
as the disjoint union

R=(\alpha\setminus\{0\})\cup-\alpha,

and we may consider the restrictions

*_{R}|_{\alpha\setminus\{0\}}:(\alpha\setminus\{0\})\times\supp(\alpha)\to R\quad\&\quad*_{R}|_{-\alpha}:-\alpha\times\supp(\alpha)\to R.

We know

\displaystyle(\alpha\setminus\{0\})*\supp(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(\alpha\setminus\{0\})*(\alpha\cap-\alpha)

\displaystyle=((\alpha\setminus\{0\})*\alpha)\cap((\alpha\setminus\{0\})*-\alpha),

and since
(\alpha\setminus\{0\})*\alpha\subseteq\alpha
and
(\alpha\setminus\{0\})*-\alpha\subseteq-\alpha
, we may restrict to obtain a multiplication

*_{R}|_{\alpha\setminus\{0\}}:(\alpha\setminus\{0\})\times\supp(\alpha)\to\supp(\alpha).

An analogous argument shows we may restrict the codomain of

*_{R}|_{-\alpha}:-\alpha\times\supp(\alpha)\to R

to obtain a multiplication

*_{R}|_{-\alpha}:-\alpha\times\supp(\alpha)\to\supp(\alpha),

and by extension of maps of sets we obtain a multiplication

*_{R}:R\times\supp(\alpha)\to\supp(\alpha).

The above shows, for the positive cone of a total order
\alpha
, that
\supp(\alpha)
is an ideal of
R
.
∎

1.2.
Cones and Homomorphisms

Theorem 5
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a homomorphism of rings, and let
\beta
be the positive cone of a partial order on
B
. Then
\alpha:=f^{-1}(\beta)
is the positive cone of a partial order on
A
.

Proof.

We know
0\in\alpha
because ring homomorphisms map 0 to 0. Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in\alpha
. We have

\displaystyle a_{1}+a_{2}\in f^{-1}(\beta)

\displaystyle\iff f(a_{1}+a_{2})\in\beta

\displaystyle\iff f(a_{1})+f(a_{2})\in\beta.

Since
a_{1},a_{2}\in f^{-1}(\beta)
,
f(a_{1})\,\&\,f(a_{2})
are in
\beta
, as is
f(a_{1})+f(a_{2})
. We have thus showed
+_{A}:\alpha\times\alpha\to\alpha
is a function, and the same argument shows
*_{A}:\alpha\times\alpha\to\alpha
is a function. Lastly,

\displaystyle a_{1}\in\alpha

\displaystyle\iff f(a_{1})\in\beta

\displaystyle\implies-f(a_{1})\notin\beta

\displaystyle\iff f(-a_{1})\notin\beta

\displaystyle\iff-a_{1}\notin f^{-1}(\beta)=\alpha,

and we’ve demonstrated positive cones of partial orders pull back to positive cones of partial orders under ring homomorphisms.
∎

Now, we might ask whether the positive cones of
total
orders on
B
are pulled back to positive cones of total orders under ring homomorphisms
f:A\to B
. In general, they are not;

Example 6
.

Consider the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra homomorphism
f:\mathbb{R}[t]\to\mathbb{R}
which maps
t\mapsto 3
.
\mathbb{R}
is totally ordered with positive cone
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and

f^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[t].

If
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[t]
is the positive cone of a
total
order, any two elements of
\mathbb{R}[t]
must be comparable under that order. For the polynomials
t^{2}+1
and
2t^{2}
,

(t^{2}+1)-(2t^{2})=1-t^{2}\notin\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[t],

and

(2t^{2})-(t^{2}+1)=-(1-t^{2})=t^{2}-1\notin\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}[t],

so the existence of this pair demonstrates that the total order
\geq
on
\mathbb{R}
only pulls back to a partial order on
\mathbb{R}[t]
via
f
.

Lemma 7
.

If
f:A\to B
is an injective ring homomorphism, and
\beta
is the positive cone of a total order on
B
,
f^{-1}(\beta)
is the positive cone of a total order on
A
.

Proof.

We already know
f^{-1}(\beta)
is the total cone of a partial order on
A
. Fix a nonzero
a\in A
, and suppose
-a\notin f^{-1}(\beta)
. We want to show
a\in f^{-1}(\beta)
We then know
f(-a)=-f(a)\notin\beta
, and consequently
f(a)\in\beta
. Since
f
is injective,
f(a)\neq 0
. If
f(a)\in\beta
,
a\in f^{-1}(\beta)
by definition, proving
f^{-1}(\beta)
is the positive cone of a total order on
A
.
∎

1.3.
Introducing Pre-Geometry

Let
f:A\to B
be a ring homomorphism, and let
\beta\subset B
such that
\beta/\supp(\beta)
is the positive cone of a total order on
B/\supp(\beta)
, and
\supp(\beta)
is prime. We will see shortly that subsets
\beta\subset B
with prime support, such that
\beta/\supp(\beta)\subset B/\supp(\beta)
totally orders
B/\supp(\beta)
, are the right objects to form a locally ringed space from! Since the inverse image of a prime ideal is prime under ring homomorphism, we obtain a commutative diagram of rings
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/f^{-1}(\supp(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B/\supp(\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\bar{f}}

Since

\displaystyle f^{-1}(\beta\cap-\beta)

\displaystyle=f^{-1}(\beta)\cap f^{-1}(-\beta)

\displaystyle=f^{-1}(\beta)\cap-f^{-1}(\beta),

the support of the cone of partial order
f^{-1}(\beta)
is the prime ideal
f^{-1}(\supp(\beta))
, and we may extend the diagram above to
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/f^{-1}(\supp(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B/\supp(\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\bar{f}}

Let
\overline{a}
denote the image of an element
a\in A
under projection, and let
\overline{\alpha}
denote the image of a subset
\alpha\subset A
under projection (and analogously for
B
). I claim
\overline{f^{-1}(\beta)}
is the positive cone of a total order on
A/\supp(f^{-1}(\beta))
. Suppose
\bar{a}\in A/\supp(f^{-1}(\beta))
with
-\bar{a}\notin\overline{f^{-1}(\beta)}
. We then have

\bar{f}(-\bar{a})\notin\bar{\beta},

and since
\bar{\beta}
totally orders
B/\supp(\beta)
,

\bar{f}(\bar{a})\in\bar{\beta}.

By commutativity of the above diargram,
f(a)\in\beta
, and so

a\in f^{-1}(\beta).

Taking equivalence classes,
\bar{a}\in\overline{f^{-1}(\beta)}
, which proves
\bar{\beta}
pulls back to the positive cone of a total order
\overline{f^{-1}(\beta)}
on
A
.

The following example illustrates the above technique with
A=R
and
f=\text{id}_{R}
:

Example 8
.

Let
\alpha\subseteq\beta\subset R
, where
\overline{\alpha}\subset R/\supp(\alpha)
totally orders the integral domain
R/\supp(\alpha)
, and
\overline{\beta}\subset R/\supp(\beta)
totally orders the integral domain
R/\supp(\beta)
. We have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\supp(\beta),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\beta}}

and since
\supp(\alpha)\subseteq\ker(\pi_{\beta})
,
we may pass
\pi_{\beta}
to the quotient to obtain a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\supp(\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi_{\beta}}}

We may now totally order
R/\supp(\alpha)
using either
\overline{\alpha}
or
\overline{\pi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\overline{\beta})
. On the one hand, if
\overline{r}\in\overline{\alpha}
, fix an equivalence class representative
r
of
\overline{r}
. Since
\alpha\subseteq\beta
,

\pi_{\beta}(r)\in\overline{\beta}\subset R/\supp(\beta).

We have now shown

\overline{\alpha}\subseteq\overline{\pi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\overline{\beta}).

If there is an
x
such that
x\in\overline{\pi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\overline{\beta})
and
x\notin\overline{\alpha}
, since
\overline{\alpha}
totally orders
R/\supp(\alpha)
, we must have
-x\in\overline{\alpha}
. We know
\overline{\alpha}\subseteq\overline{\pi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\overline{\beta})
, so
\{x,-x\}\subset\overline{\pi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\overline{\beta})
, forcing
x=0
. As
0

is
an element of
\overline{\alpha}
by definition, we have reached a contradiction and

\overline{\alpha}=\overline{\pi_{\beta}}^{-1}(\overline{\beta}).

1.4.
The Topologies of Sper(-)

We can now define the points of
\sper(A)
for any commutative unital ring
A
.

Definition 9
.

(
[
8
]
,2) Let
A
be a commutative unital ring. The elements (read: points) of the real spectrum
\text{Sper}(A)
are subsets
\alpha\subset A
such that

(1)

\supp(\alpha)=\alpha\cap-\alpha
is a prime ideal of
A
, and

(2)

\alpha/\supp(\alpha)
is the positive cone of a total order on
A/\supp(\alpha)
.

We note that there is a map
\supp:\sper(A)\to\text{Spec}(A)
given by
\alpha\mapsto\alpha\cap-\alpha
. I like to think of the image of
\supp
as ’the set of prime ideals which are boundaries of positive cones of total order’. As we shall see in dealing with semialgebraic sets over a real closed field, the prime ideals correspond to varieties, and positive cones of total order correspond to regions these real varieties bound.

For the duration of this paper, wherever I specify a ring, I intend it to be commutative and unital. I may be less precise in my algebra moving forward, sometimes using the notation
a\in A/\supp(\alpha)
, for some
\alpha\in\sper(A)
, to mean
\pi_{\alpha}(a)\in A/\supp(\alpha)
. Fix a ring
A
. To define a topology on
\sper(A)
, basic open sets in
\sper(A)
should be given by the nonvanishing of some ’function’. We will define the weak topology on
\sper(A)
which is analogous to the Zariski topology, and the proconstructible topology which is analogous to the smallest topology on
\sper(A)
containing the constructible subsets of
\sper(A)
in the weak topology as closed sets. We explain the weak topology first, as it is required to define the proconstructible topology.

Let
a\in A
and
\alpha\in\sper(A)
. We wish to evaluate ’the function’
a
at the point
\alpha\in\sper(A)
. We have a ring homomorphism
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(A/\supp(\alpha)\right)_{\langle 0\rangle},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\alpha}}

and we fix the notation

\kappa(\alpha):=\left(A/\supp(\alpha)\right)_{\langle 0\rangle}.

I claim
\kappa(\alpha)
is a totally ordered field, and I will show this by extending the total ordering on
A/\supp(\alpha)
. Fix distinct
a/b,c/d\in\kappa(\alpha)
, and define the following total order on
\kappa(\alpha)
;

\frac{c}{d}\preceq\frac{a}{b}\iff ad-bc\in\overline{\alpha}=\pi_{\alpha}(\alpha).

Since either
ad-bc\in\overline{\alpha}
or
bc-ad\in\overline{\alpha}
, and both are in
\overline{\alpha}
precisely when

\displaystyle ad-bc=0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle ad=bc\;\text{ in }A/\supp(\alpha)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{a}{b}=\frac{c}{d}\;\text{ in }\kappa(\alpha).

I leave it as an exercise to show
\preceq
has transitivity, and that the properties of addition and multiplication by an element of the positive cone play well with this ordering.

We have now established
\kappa(\alpha)
is a totally ordered field, so by The Artin-Schreier Theorem, there is a unique algebraic extension
\kappa(\alpha)\subseteq\rho(\alpha)
, contained in the algebraic closure of
\kappa(\alpha)
such that

•

\rho(\alpha)
is a totally ordered field, and the ordering of
\rho(\alpha)
extends the ordering of
\kappa(\alpha)
,

•

for all
x\in\rho(\alpha)
with
0\preceq x
, there is an element
y\in\rho(\alpha)
such that
y^{2}=x
.

•

Every polynomial
f(t)\in\rho(\alpha)[t]
of odd degree has a root in
\rho(\alpha)
.

We call ordered fields of this kind
real closed fields
, and there are many equivalent definitions of them. We call the final property the
intermediate value property
. As examples,
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}_{alg}:=\mathbb{R}\cap\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
are both real closed fiels. Please see pages 7-17 in
[
1
]
for further fundamental results on real closed fields.

We may now extend our previous diagram;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}

We think of
\rho_{\alpha}(a)
as the evaluation of the function
a
at the point
\alpha
, and denote it
a(\alpha)
.

Let
R
be a subring of
\rho(\alpha)
. Since
\rho(\alpha)
is a ring containing
R
with the intermediate value property, by Zorn’s Lemma, there is a smallest ring
C(R)
such that
R\subseteq C(R)\subseteq\rho(\alpha)
and
C(R)
has the intermediate value property.

Definition 10
.

If
R
is a subring of a real closed field
\rho
, the
convex hull

C(R)
of
R
in
\rho
is the smallest subring (by containment) of
\rho
containing
R
in which the intermediate value property holds. Generally, we may describe any totally ordered ring
C
in which the intermediate value property holds as
convex
.

Convex ideals are especially well-behaved in convex rings;

Proposition 11
.

Let
C
be a convex ring with positive cone
C_{+}
, and let
I
be a nonzero convex ideal of
C
. Then there exists a ring element
b\in C
such that

-b\leq x\leq b

for all
x\in I
.

Proof.

Firstly, note that if
b\in C
bounds
I
from above, then
-b
bounds it from below, so it is sufficient to show
I
has an upper bound. Towards a contradiction, suppose
I
is unbounded. Let
a\in C_{+}
, and since
I
is unbounded, choose
b\in I
such that
b\geq a
. Since

a\in C_{+},\,b-a\in C_{+},\,\text{ and }b\in I

by convexity of
I
,
a\in I
. We chose
a
arbitrarily, and so

1\in C_{+}\subseteq I,

a contradiction.
∎

Perhaps more impressively, the convex prime ideals form a chain in any convex ring;

Proposition 12
.

Let
C
be a convex ring with positive cone
C_{+}
, and suppose
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
are nonzero convex prime ideals. Then either
p_{0}\subseteq p_{1}
or
p_{1}\subseteq p_{0}
.

Proof.

We first use a simple algorithm to determine which prime contains the other. The proof, following the algorithm, is straightforward.

i.

Without loss of generality, fix
x\in p_{0}
,

ii.

If
x\geq y
for all
y\in p_{1}
, return
x
.

iii.

If not, there is an element
z_{1}\in p_{1}
such that
z_{1}>x
.

iv.

If
z_{1}\geq y
for all
y\in p_{0}
, return
z_{1}
.

v.

If not, there is a
z_{2}\in p_{0}
such that
z_{2}>z_{1}

vi.

Update
z_{2}\mapsto x
and proceed from ii.

The algorithm above produces greater and greater elements of
p_{1}\cup p_{2}
, and since
p_{i}
is bounded for all
i
by the previous proposition, so is
p_{0}\cup p_{1}
. Since the order topology on
C
is Hausdorff and
C
is convex, the
z_{i}
’s have a limit and the algorithm terminates.
Let
x_{\infty}\in C
denote the element on which the algorithm terminates.
Since
x_{\infty}\in p_{0}\cup p_{1}
, without loss of generality suppose
x_{\infty}\in p_{1}
. I claim that in this case,
p_{0}\subseteq p_{1}
. If
a\in p_{0}
, one of
a
and
-a
are in
C_{+}
, so we assume
a\geq 0
for ease of notation. By convexity, since

a\in C_{+},\,x_{\infty}-a\in C_{+},\text{ and }x_{\infty}\in p_{1},

we know
a\in p_{1}
, proving this case.
If
x_{\infty}\notin p_{0}\cup p_{1}
,
x_{\infty}
must be the least upper bound for
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
, implying that

p_{0}=(-x_{\infty},x_{\infty})=p_{1},

concluding the second and final case in this proof.
∎

Let

D(a)=\{\alpha\in\sper(A):a(\alpha)>0\}.

If
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
, then

D(a_{1})\cap D(a_{2})=\{\alpha\in\sper(A):a_{1}(\alpha)>0\text{ and }a_{2}(\alpha)>0\},

which is defined to be
D(a_{1},a_{2})
. Similarly, for any collection of elements
\{a_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\subset A
, we have

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}D(a_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\sper(A):\exists\lambda\in\Lambda\;s.t.\;a_{\lambda}(\alpha)>0\},

but there may not exist an element
\hat{a}\in A
such that

D(\hat{a})=\{\alpha\in\sper(A):\exists\lambda\in\Lambda\;s.t.\;a_{\lambda}(\alpha)>0\}.

Example 13
.

Let
\rho
be the real closure of the ordered field
\mathbb{Q}
in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
, and let
A=\rho[t]
. Let

\{a_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}=\left\{t-\frac{1}{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}.

In
\sper(\rho[t])
, we then compute

\displaystyle\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}D\left(t-\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\sper(\rho[t]):\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\;s.t.\;a_{n}(\alpha)>0\}.

We observe

D\left(t-\frac{1}{n}\right)=\left\{\alpha\in\sper(\rho[t]):\overline{t-\frac{1}{n}}\in\overline{\alpha}\subseteq\rho[t]/\supp(\alpha)\right\},

as
\rho[t]/\supp(\alpha)
embeds in its real closure
\rho(\alpha)
as an ordered ring. As
1/n
is positive for any natural
n
, we know

\left\{\alpha\in\sper(\rho[t]):\overline{t-\frac{1}{n}}\in\overline{\alpha}\subseteq\rho[t]/\supp(\alpha)\right\}

is contained in

\left\{\alpha\in\sper(\rho[t]):\overline{t-\frac{1}{n}}+\overline{\frac{1}{n}}\in\overline{\alpha}\subseteq\rho[t]/\supp(\alpha)\right\},

or more succinctly

D\left(t-\frac{1}{n}\right)\subseteq D(t)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence the union over all natural
n
is also contained in
D(t)
;

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}D\left(t-\frac{1}{n}\right)\subseteq D(t).

Let

\alpha_{0}=\{f(t)\in\rho[t]:f(0)\geq 0\}.

We then have
\alpha_{0}\in D(t)
by definition, but

\overline{\left(t-\frac{1}{n}\right)}(\alpha_{0})=\overline{\left(-\frac{1}{n}\right)}(\alpha_{0})<0

in
\rho[t]/\langle t\rangle
for all natural
n
. Hence

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}D\left(t-\frac{1}{n}\right)\subset D(t).

Observing that
D(1)=\sper(A)
and
D(-1)=\emptyset
, these subsets of
D(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\subseteq\sper(A)
form a basis for a topology on
\sper(A)
;

Definition 14
.

(
[
8
]
,4) The set
\sper(A)
, together with the topology generated by subsets of
\sper(A)
of the form
D(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
, is called the
real spectrum
of
A
, and this topology is called the
weak topology
.

I will sometimes write

P(a):=\{\alpha\in\sper(A):a(\alpha)\geq 0\}=D(-a)^{c}.

The
constructible
subsets of the real spectrum are those which can be written using using basis open sets and finitely many applications of union, intersection, and complement. For example

P(a)\cap D(a)=D(a)

is constructible in
\sper(A)
.

Definition 15
.

The smallest topology on
\sper(A)
containing the constructible subsets of
\sper(A)
in the weak topology as
closed
sets is called the
proconstructible topology
on
\sper(A)
.

If I do not specify a topology, I always mean the weak topology.
If
f:R\to A
is a ring homomorphism, we have shown previously that for subsets
\alpha\subset A
which project to positive cones of total orders under
A\to A/\supp(\alpha)
, we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R/\supp(f^{-1}(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists\bar{f}}

In the language of topology, this means we have a map of sets

\phi:\sper(A)\to\sper(R),

Proposition 16
.

If
f:R\to A
is a ring homomorphism, and
\phi:\sper(A)\to\sper(R)
is the corresponding map of sets, then
\phi
is continuous in the weak topology.

Proof.

Let
r
be a nonzero element of
R
, and consider the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(r),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

which we may extend to a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{-1}(D(r))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(r).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi|_{\phi^{-1}(D(r))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

We have

\displaystyle\phi^{-1}(D(r))=

\displaystyle\{\alpha\in\sper(A):r(\phi(\alpha))>0\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\alpha\in\sper(A):r(f^{-1}(\alpha))>0\},

and we will proceed to show this set is a basis open set in
\sper(A)
. Now,
r(f^{-1}(\alpha))>0
holds precisely when
r\in f^{-1}(\alpha)/\supp(f^{-1}(\alpha))
and
r\neq 0
. We then know

f(r)\in\alpha/\supp(\alpha),

and so

\displaystyle\{\alpha\in\sper(A):r(f^{-1}(\alpha))>0\}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha\in\sper(A):f(r)(\alpha)>0\}

\displaystyle=D(f(r)),

proving not only that
\phi^{-1}(D(r))
is open, but also that it equals
D(f(r))
!
∎

Definition 17
.

(
[
8
]
,4) For rings
A
and
B
, a map
\varphi:\sper(A)\to\sper(B)
is a
morphism of spectral spaces
if it is continuous in the weak and proconstructible topologies.

As you might expect, if
f:B\to A
is a ring homomorphism, the corresponding map
\varphi:\sper(A)\to\sper(B)
is a morphism of spectral spaces.

Now, we might wonder about fibers over points. For starters, if
\rho_{\alpha}:A\to\rho(\alpha)
is the evaluation morphism defined previously,

\rho_{\alpha}^{-1}(\{x\in\rho(\alpha):x\geq 0\})=\alpha,

so this yields a bijective correspondence between points
\alpha\in\sper(A)
and spaces
\sper(\rho(\alpha))
. Moving forward, we identify
\alpha
(with the subspace topology) and
\sper(\rho(\alpha))
.

It turns out the easiest way to see that
all
fiber products of affine real spectra exist is by studying the real closure operation on a ring
A
as a left-adjoint to a certain forgetful functor.

1.5.
A Model-Theoretic Moment

We have a model-theoretic way of dealing with proconstructible subsets of
\sper A
which is a bit nicer than working with the definition. Let
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}
be the language of ordered fields, and let Th be the theory of real closed fields in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}
. We may extend
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}
by adding the constants

\{c_{a}:a\in A\}

to it to obtain
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
. We may then extend the functions ’
+
’ and ’
*
’ in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
so that for all
a,b\in A
,

c_{0}=0,\;c_{1}=1,\;c_{a}+c_{b}=c_{a+b},\text{ and }c_{a}*c_{b}=c_{ab}.

A model of
\text{Th}(A)
in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
is a real closed field
F
, such that for each
a,b\in A
, there exist constants
c_{a},c_{b}\in F
such that

c_{0}=0,\;c_{1}=1,\;c_{a}+c_{b}=c_{a+b},\text{ and }c_{a}*c_{b}=c_{ab}.

Consequently, we may also think of a model of
\text{Th}(A)
in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
as a homomorphism
\rho:A\to F
to a real closed field
F
.

If
F_{+}
denotes the positive cone of total order for
F
, by Theorem 5,
\rho^{-1}(F_{+})
is the positive cone of a partial order on
A
, and it descends to a total order on
A/\ker(\rho)
. Hence
\rho^{-1}(F_{+})
is a point in
\sper A
, and conversely, each point
\alpha\in\sper A
gives a homomorphism to a real closed field
\rho_{\alpha}:A\to\rho(\alpha)
. So the points of
\sper A
are in bijection with the collection of homomorphisms
\rho:A\to F
modulo the relation

(\rho_{1}:A\to E)\sim(\rho_{2}:A\to F)\iff\rho_{1}^{-1}(E_{+})=\rho_{2}^{-1}(F_{+}).

We can also describe constructible subsets of
\sper A
using model theory. Fix
a\in A
, and consider the open set

D(a)\subset\sper A.

We know

D(a)=\{\alpha\in\sper A:a(\alpha)>0\},

and
a(\alpha)=\rho_{\alpha}(a)
, where
\rho_{\alpha}:A\to\rho(\alpha)
is the evaluation map. For each
\alpha\in D(a)
, consider the equivalence class of homomorphisms
(\rho_{\alpha}:A\to\rho(\alpha))
that satisfy the sentence

\varphi:\;c_{a}>0

parameterized by
a
in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
. Under the previous correspondence,

D(a)=\{(\rho:A\to F):\rho\models\varphi\},

and more generally, since constructible subsets are finite boolean combinations of sets of the above form, they can also be described by sentences
\varphi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})
with finitely many parameters
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
from
A
. Intersections of constructible sets correspond to combining sentences with
\wedge
, so infinite intersections
1
1

1

These are the interesting pro-constructible subsets!
correspond to ’infinite-length’ sentences in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
.

If
B\to A
is a ring homomorphism, (pro)constructible subsets
L\subseteq\sper B
have (pro)constructible inverse images
K\subseteq\sper A
under the induced morphism of spectral spaces. However, (pro)constructible subsets of
\sper A
are not necessarily sent to (pro)constructible subsets of
\sper B
under the induced map of spectral spaces. In some fairly general cases, however, we get lucky and such subsets are preserved as described below.

Let
f:R\to A
be a homomorphism with induced map
\pi:\sper A\to\sper R
such that
A
is a finitely presented algebra over
R
;

R\to A\cong R[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/\langle Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}\rangle.

Let
\varphi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})
be a sentence in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
with parameters
a_{i}
describing a constructible subset
K
of
\sper A
. Each polynomial
a_{i}
has coefficients
a_{i,1},\ldots,a_{i,\deg(a_{i})}
in
R
, and similarly for the
Q_{i}
. Let
\Phi
be the sentence over
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(R)
, with the coefficients of the
a_{i}
and
Q_{j}
as parameters, and in the variables
X_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
which says

\exists X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}:\varphi(a_{1}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}),\ldots,a_{m}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}))\wedge\left(\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}Q_{j}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=0\right).

I claim this sentence exactly describes
\pi(K)
. If
(R\to\rho(\beta))
is in
\pi(K)
, there exists a point
(A\to\rho(\alpha))
in
K
such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\beta}}

commutes. Consequently, we have a commutative diagram of ring homomorphisms
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}

Since
R\to\rho(\alpha)
factors through
A\to\rho(\alpha)
and
(A\to\rho(\alpha))
satisfies
\varphi
,
(R\to\rho(\alpha))
satisfies
\Phi
, proving that the elements of
\pi(K)
satisfy
\Phi
. Suppose
(R\to\rho(\beta))
satisfies
\Phi
. Using elementary ring theory, we can construct a homomorphism
\lambda:A\to\rho(\beta)
such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

commutes, where
\lambda(x_{i})=X_{i}
in
\rho(\beta)
. Hence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

commutes, and
(R\to\rho(\beta))
is in
\pi(K)
. We have now proved

Theorem 18
.

(
[
2
]
,34) Let
f:R\to A
be a ring homomorphism giving a finite presentation of
A
over
R
. Let
\pi:\sper A\to\sper R
be the induced morphism of spectral spaces, and let
K
be a (pro)constructible subset of
\sper A
. Then
\pi(K)
is (pro)constructible.

1.6.
A Sheaf on Sper(-)

We are ready to define the structure sheaf of the real spectrum!
Let
A
be a ring with real spectrum
X:=\sper(A)
in its weak topology. The structure homomorphism
p:A\to A[T]
yields a morphism of spectral spaces
\pi:\sper A[T]\to\sper A
. The set of morphisms of spectral spaces

\{\sigma:\sper A\to\sper A[T]:\pi\circ\sigma=\text{id}_{\sper A}\}

corresponds bijectively to the evaluation maps

e_{a}:A[T]\to A\;\text{ defined by }T\mapsto a.

These morphisms
e_{a}
have a natural ring structure; for
a,b\in A
, we have

\displaystyle e_{a}+e_{b}=

\displaystyle e_{a+b}\quad\text{, and }

\displaystyle e_{a}e_{b}=

\displaystyle e_{ab}.

Hence sections of
\pi:\sper A[T]\to\sper A
have a ring structure which contains a ring isomorphic to
A
.

Let
K\subseteq\sper A
be a proconstructible subset of
\sper A
.
\pi^{-1}(K)
is the inverse image of an intersection of constructible sets
\bigcap_{\gamma\in\Gamma}C_{\gamma}
, and since constructible sets are preserved under inverse image,

\pi^{-1}(K)=\pi^{-1}\left(\bigcap_{\gamma\in\Gamma}C_{\gamma}\right)=\bigcap_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\pi^{-1}(C_{\gamma})

is proconstructible. Consider the ’local sections’

\left\{\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)\biggr\lvert\sigma(K)\text{ is closed constructible, and }(\pi\lvert_{\pi^{-1}(K)})\circ\sigma=\text{id}_{K}\right\}.

In the paragraphs to come, we will see these sections carry a ring structure just like the global sections
[
8
]
!

Definition 19
.

Let
X=\sper A
. Define

\mathscr{O}_{X}(X):=\{\sigma:X\to\sper A[T]:\pi\circ\sigma=\text{id}_{X}\}

as the
global sections of the structure sheaf of

\sper A
, and define

\mathscr{O}_{X}(K):=\left\{\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)\biggr\lvert\sigma(K)\text{ is closed constructible, and }\pi\lvert_{\pi^{-1}(K)}\circ\sigma=\text{id}_{K}\right\}

as the
local sections of the structure sheaf

\mathscr{O}_{X}
.

If
\alpha
is a point in
K
, a proconstructible subset of
\sper A
, by Proposition 4.3 of
[
2
]
, we have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A[t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{-1}(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\alpha)[t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\alpha).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi|}

If
\sigma\in\mathscr{O}_{\sper A}(K)
, then

\{\sigma(\alpha)\}_{\alpha\in K}=\sigma(K)\cap\pi^{-1}(\alpha)

is closed constructible in
\sper\rho(\alpha)[t]
. If
r\in\rho(\alpha)
, we have a corresponding subspace

\{\gamma\in\sper\rho(\alpha)[t]:t(\gamma)=r\},

and since we can recover
r
by evaluating
t
at any point in the set, the correspondence is bijective. Under this correspondence,
\sigma(\alpha)\in\rho(\alpha)
, and

\sigma(K)\subseteq\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha).

Moving forward, we will also denote the map

K\to\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)

by
\sigma
. Letting
X=\sper A
, we obtain a map

i:\mathscr{O}_{X}(K)\to\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)

which takes
\sigma
and maps it to

(\sigma(\alpha))_{\alpha\in K}.

Since different sections
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
must take different values at some point
\gamma\in K
,
i
is an injective
set map
of rings. We aim to show
\mathscr{O}_{X}(K)
is a subring of
\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)

[
8
]
.

Before proceeding, we need to know that if
\alpha,\beta\in K
are points such that
\alpha\subseteq\beta
, we have
\sigma(\alpha)=\sigma(\beta)
(in some sensible field). In the paragraphs below, we develop the algebraic machinery to guarantee exactly this.

Definition 20
.

(
[
8
]
,6) Fix a ring
A
, and let
\alpha,\beta\in\sper(A)
. We say
\alpha

specializes to

\beta
, or
\beta

is a specialization of

\alpha
if
\beta
is in the closure of
\alpha
in the weak topology, or
\alpha\subseteq\beta
. We similarly say
\alpha
is a
generization
of
\beta
.

Lemma 21
.

(
[
8
]
,6) Let
\alpha,\beta\in\sper A
, with
\beta
a specialization of
\alpha
. Then
\,\textbf{}\supp(\beta)/\supp(\alpha)
is a convex ideal in
A/\supp(\alpha)
.

Proof.

Let
a
and
b
be elements of
A/\supp(\alpha)
, and suppose
a
and
b-a
are in
\alpha/\supp(\alpha)
, and
b\in I=\supp(\beta)/\supp(\alpha)
. We want to show
a\in I
. We have a projection

p:A/\supp(\alpha)\to A/\supp(\beta),

and we use this projection to analyze the cases
a\neq 0
and
a=0
separately. If
a=0
,
a\in I
because
I
is an ideal. If
a\neq 0
,

p(\{a,b-a\})=\{p(a),p(b-a)\}=\{p(a),-p(a)\}\subset\beta/\supp(\beta),

and since
\beta/\supp(\beta)
totally orders
A/\supp(\beta)
,
p(a)=0
. Hence
a\in I
.
∎

Now, given
\alpha
and
\beta
as in the above lemma, we have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}

which commutes since
\alpha\subseteq\beta
. By Zorn, there is a largest convex subring
C_{\beta\alpha}\subseteq\rho(\alpha)
with maximal ideal
m_{\beta\alpha}
such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}

commutes, and

f^{-1}(m_{\beta\alpha})=\supp(\beta)/\supp(\alpha).

I claim
\rho_{\beta\alpha}=C_{\beta\alpha}/m_{\beta\alpha}
is a real closed field, and by
[
6
]
, it suffices to show
\rho_{\beta\alpha}
is uniquely and maximally ordered, in such a way that with this ordering,
\rho_{\beta\alpha}
is an ordered field. Since
C_{\beta\alpha}
is convex in
\rho(\alpha)
, the positive cone
C_{+}\subset C_{\beta\alpha}
is simply

C_{+}=C_{\beta\alpha}\cap\{y\in\rho(\alpha):\exists x\in\rho(\alpha)\text{ such that }x^{2}=y\}.

In the quotient
C_{\beta\alpha}\to\rho_{\beta\alpha}
,
C_{+}/m_{\beta\alpha}
remains closed under addition and multiplication, so it is left to check that at most one of
x
and
-x
are in
C_{+}/m_{\beta\alpha}
for all
x\in
. Without loss of generality, assume
-x\notin C_{+}/m_{\beta\alpha}
. We then have

\displaystyle-x\notin C_{+}/m_{\beta\alpha}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle-x+a\notin C_{+}\text{ for all }a\in m_{\beta\alpha}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle-x\notin C_{+}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle x\in C_{+}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle x\in C_{+}/m_{\beta\alpha},

which implies
C_{+}/m_{\beta\alpha}
is the positive cone of a total order on
\rho_{\beta\alpha}
. Since
C_{\beta\alpha}\subseteq\rho(\alpha)
, and
\rho(\alpha)
is uniquely and maximally ordered,
C_{\beta\alpha}
is uniquely and maximally ordered. Hence the quotient
\rho_{\beta\alpha}
is maximally ordered with positive cone equal to its squares, so it is real closed by
[
6
]
.

We may now extend our diagram to
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\beta\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}

and since
\supp(\beta)
is in the kernel of

A/\supp(\alpha)\to C_{\beta\alpha}\to\rho_{\beta\alpha},

passing to the quotient by
\supp(\beta)
yields a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\beta\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}

By the universal properties of the fraction field and of real closed fields,
A/\supp(\beta)\to\rho_{\beta\alpha}
factors through
\rho(\beta)
to yield the final commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\beta\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

Remark 22
.

Suppose
A=\mathbb{Q}[x,y]
, and define
\alpha\in\sper A
as

\alpha=\left\{f\in A:\exists\text{ rational }\epsilon>0\text{ such that }f\biggr\lvert_{[0,\epsilon]\times[0,\epsilon]}\geq 0\right\},

and
\beta\in\sper A
as

\beta=\{f\in A:f(0,0)\geq 0\}.

We have by definition that
\beta
is a specialization of
\alpha
, i.e. that
\alpha\subset\beta
. Using our model theoretic definition of
\sper A
,
\alpha
corresponds to the homomorphism
\mathbb{Q}[x,y]\to\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}(\epsilon)
with
x,y\mapsto\epsilon
, and
\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}(\epsilon)
is the smallest real closed field containing
\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
and some
\epsilon
greater than zero and smaller than every other positive real algebraic number. The point
\beta\in\sper A
corresponds to the homomorphism
\mathbb{Q}[x,y]\to\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
given by evaluation at
(0,0)
. Note that
there are no field homomorphisms

\rho(\alpha)\to\rho(\beta)
! Even though there is a real closed field homomorphism

\rho(\beta)=\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}(\epsilon)=\rho(\alpha),

this homomorphism does not come from the geometry or topology of
\sper A
.
To see where this homomorphism is coming from, note that we can factor the homomorphisms
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}[x,y]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}(\epsilon)}

as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}[x,y]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}[x,y]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}(\epsilon).}

Since the surjective homomorphism
\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}[x,y]\to\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}
has a splitting, we get the homomorphism
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}[x,y]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\text{alg}}(\epsilon).}

When working with rings of finite type over ordered fields, there are many other non-geometric coincidences that occur, such as consequences of the Tarski-Seidenberg principle, or getting a morphism from the universal property of real closed fields.

If points can live within other points in the real spectrum, the analogue of a continuous function should be reasonable with respect to the lattice structure of these points;

Definition 23
.

(
[
8
]
,7) Let
K
be a (pro)constructible subset of
\sper A
. An element
\sigma
of the ring
\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
is a
compatible section
if for all
\alpha
and
\beta
, with
\beta
a specialization of
\alpha
, we have

(1)

\sigma(\alpha)\in C_{\beta\alpha}
, and

(2)

\pi_{\beta\alpha}(\sigma(\alpha))=\iota(\sigma(\beta))
in
\rho_{\beta\alpha}
.

Let
\sigma_{id}:K\to\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
send
\alpha\in K
to
1\in\rho(\alpha)
. Then, for each
\alpha,\beta\in K
with
\beta\subseteq\overline{\alpha}
, we know

\sigma_{id}(\alpha)=1\in C_{\beta\alpha}.

We also have

\pi_{\beta\alpha}(1)=\iota(1)=1,

so compatible sections exist, and
\sigma_{id}
is a compatible section.

In fact, we can show each element of
A
gives a compatible section! Since
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\beta\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

commutes from
A
, the diagram commutes for every
\alpha,\beta\in K
with
\beta\in\overline{\alpha}
, and elements of
\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
which correspond to elements of
A
via

A\to\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)

are all compatible sections.

Let
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are compatible sections on a constructible subspace
K\subseteq\sper A
, and consider their sum
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}
. For each
\alpha,\beta\in K
with
\beta\in\bar{\alpha}
, we have

(\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})(\alpha)=\sigma_{1}(\alpha)+\sigma_{2}(\alpha)\in C_{\beta\alpha},

and

\displaystyle\pi_{\beta\alpha}((\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})(\alpha))=

\displaystyle\pi_{\beta\alpha}(\sigma_{1}(\alpha))+\pi_{\beta\alpha}(\sigma_{2}(\alpha))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota(\sigma_{1}(\beta))+\iota(\sigma_{2}(\beta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota((\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2})(\beta))

Hence the sum of compatible sections is compatible, and continuing in a similar manner, one can prove

Corollary 24
.

(
[
8
]
,7) The set of compatible sections
\sigma\in\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
is a ring.

Theorem 25
.

(
[
8
]
,7) We consider
\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
as a subset of
\pi^{-1}(K)\subseteq\sper A[t]
as before, where
\pi:\sper A[t]\to\sper A
is induced by the
A
-algebra structure homomorphism. An element
\sigma\in\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
is a compatible section if and only if the following condition holds:

If
\alpha,\beta\in K
with
\beta\in\overline{\alpha}
,
\sigma(\beta)
is the unique specialization of
\sigma(\alpha)
in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
.

Proof.

Before we prove the above, let’s establish some crucial facts. Since
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is a local section of
\pi
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{-1}(K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\sigma(\alpha)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

commutes, and since
\pi(\sigma(\alpha))=\alpha
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\sigma(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

also commutes. On the level of ring homomorphisms, we have
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\sigma(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}

proving
\rho(\alpha)=\rho(\sigma(\alpha))
. We then have a diagram of
A
-algebra homomorphisms
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}}

which commutes because
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A[t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\rho(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

does. The element
\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}(t)\in\sper\rho(\alpha)
must be in the image of
\rho_{\alpha}
, because the diagram still commutes if we include the homomorphism induced by the local section
\sigma
;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}}

Consequently, when
\sigma(\alpha)
is considered an element of
\rho(\alpha)
, it equals
\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}(t)
by commutativity.

Now, let
\sigma\in\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
be a compatible section, and recall the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\beta\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

where
\alpha,\beta\in K
and
\beta\in\bar{\alpha}
. We have a corresponding commutative diagram for
\sigma(\beta)
, which is contained in
\overline{\sigma(\alpha)}
;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]/\supp(\sigma(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]/\supp(\sigma(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

We then have

\displaystyle\iota\circ\rho_{\sigma(\beta)}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota(\sigma(\beta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}(\sigma(\alpha))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}\circ\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}(t),

and
\sigma(\beta)
and
\sigma(\alpha)
are equal as elements of
\rho_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}
. It is left to show that this specialization is unique.

Suppose
\gamma\in\pi^{-1}(\beta)
is such that
\gamma\in\overline{\sigma(\alpha)}
. Since

\{\sigma(\beta)\}=\sigma(K)\cap\pi^{-1}(\beta),

and
\sigma(K)
is closed constructible in
\sper A[t]
,
\{\sigma(\beta)\}
is closed in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
. By
[
8
]
, the specializations of any point, and specifically of
\sigma(\alpha)\in\sper A[t]
, form a chain ordered by inclusion, and so we must have

\sigma(\alpha)\subseteq\gamma\subseteq\sigma(\beta).

Let
C_{\gamma\sigma(\alpha)}
be the largest convex subring of
\rho(\sigma(\alpha))=\rho(\alpha)
with a maximal ideal
m_{\gamma}
such that

\rho_{\alpha}^{-1}(m_{\gamma})=\supp(\gamma)/\supp(\sigma(\alpha)).

We know
\supp(\gamma)\subseteq\supp(\sigma(\beta))
, and so the largest convex subring
C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}
with a maximal ideal
m_{\sigma(\beta)}
must contain the largest convex subring
C_{\gamma\sigma(\alpha)}
with maximal ideal
m_{\gamma}
. Since convex subrings of real closed fields are valuation rings by (
[
9
]
, 6) , It follows that
\gamma\in\overline{\sigma(\beta)}
, implying they are equal, and that specializations are unique in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
.

Now, we will show that if
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is a morphism of spectral spaces such that for all
\alpha,\beta\in K
with
\alpha\subseteq\beta
,
\sigma(\beta)
is the only specialization of
\sigma(\alpha)
in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
, then
\sigma
is a compatible section. For
\alpha
and
\beta
with
\beta\supseteq\alpha
, we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]/\supp(\sigma(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Since both
C_{\beta\alpha}
and
C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}
are convex subrings of
\rho(\alpha)
with maximal ideals that have inverse image equal to

\supp(\sigma(\beta))/\supp(\sigma(\alpha))\subset A[t]/\supp(\sigma(\alpha))

under this diagram,

C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}\subseteq C_{\beta\alpha}.

Arguing towards a contradiction, suppose

C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}\neq C_{\beta\alpha}.

Let
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]/\supp(\sigma(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta\alpha}}

be a homomorphism mapping
t
to an element of
C_{\beta\alpha}\setminus C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}
. Then
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]/\supp(\sigma(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}}

must yield a different specialization, contradicting our assumptions. Hence

C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}=C_{\beta\alpha},

and as a consequence,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\beta\alpha}=C_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]/\supp(\sigma(\alpha))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[t]/\supp(\sigma(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\sigma(\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

commutes for all
\alpha\in K
with specialization
\beta\in K
. We have thus proved
\sigma
is compatible, and we are done.
∎

This theorem is powerful, as it is half of the work necessary to establish
\mathscr{O}_{X}(K)
is a ring. As an application, suppose
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is compatible as before, and further suppose

P(s):=\{\alpha\in K:\sigma(\alpha)\geq 0\}

is constructible in
K
. Let
\beta
be a specialization of
\alpha
. We then have

\iota\circ\rho_{\sigma(\beta)}(t)=\pi_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}\circ\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}(t),

and since
\iota
and
\pi_{\sigma(\beta)\sigma(\alpha)}
are both order preserving,

\sigma(\beta)=\rho_{\sigma(\beta)}(t)\geq 0.

Hence
\beta\in P(s)
, and since
P(s)
is closed under specialization and constructible,
P(s)
is closed.

The other half of proving
\mathscr{O}_{X}(K)
is a ring lies in establishing a clean way to work with morphisms with constructible image. As seen previously, working with constructible subsets is simplest with the machinery of model theory, so that is the machinery we shall use;

Definition 26
.

(
[
8
]
,8) Let
\pi:\sper A[t]\to\sper A
be induced by the
A
-algebra structure homomorphism, and let
K\subseteq\sper A
be (pro)constructible. A local section
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
of
\pi
is a
constructible section
if
\sigma(K)
is constructible in
\pi^{-1}(K)

Proposition 27
.

(
[
8
]
,8) A local section
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
of
\pi
is constructible if and only if there is some formula
\Phi(T)
in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
with variable
T
such that

•

\Phi(\sigma(\alpha))
, and

•

\exists x:\Phi(x)\,\wedge\,(\,\forall y\text{ such that }\Phi(y)\implies x=y)

holds for all
\alpha\in K
.

Proof.

First, suppose
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is a constructible section of
\pi
. Let

\phi(p_{1}(t),\ldots,p_{r}(t))

be a sentence in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A[t])
defining
\sigma(K)\subseteq\pi^{-1}(K)
. Let
\Phi
be the sentence

\exists T:\phi(p_{1}(T),\ldots,p_{r}(T))

in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
with free variable
T
. We then have

\Phi(\sigma(\alpha))=\phi(p_{1}(\sigma(\alpha)),\ldots,p_{r}(\sigma(\alpha)))=\phi(p_{1}(\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}(t)),\ldots,p_{r}(\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}(t))),

which is a true sentence in
\rho(\alpha)=\rho(\sigma(\alpha))
. We also know
\rho_{\sigma(\alpha)}(t)\in\rho(\alpha)
is the unique element of
\rho(\alpha)
satisfying
\Phi
, since the image of
\alpha
under
\sigma
uniquely specifies the image of
t
under
A[t]\to\rho(\alpha)
.

Now suppose there is a sentence
\Phi(T)
in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
with variable
T
such that

•

\Phi(\sigma(\alpha))
, and

•

\exists x:\Phi(x)\,\wedge\,(\,\forall y\text{ such that }\Phi(y)\implies x=y)

holds for all
\alpha\in K
. We hope to prove
\sigma(K)
is constructible in
\pi^{-1}(K)
, so it suffices to find a sentence
\phi
in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A[t])
which describes
\sigma(K)
. Define
\phi(t)
in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A[t])
to be
\Phi(t)
. The two defining properties of
\Phi(T)
are inherited by
\phi(t)
. The first property of
\phi(t)
guarantees that for each
\alpha
,
\phi(\sigma(\alpha))
holds, and second property assures us that
\sigma(\alpha)
is the unique input for which
\phi(t)
holds. Hence
\phi(t)
describes
\sigma(K)
and the claim is proven.
∎

We see the sentence

\Phi_{a}(T):\exists T\,\text{ such that }\,T-a=0

in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
describes the image of the
a\in A
section, so by Proposition 27 it is constructible. If
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
are constructible sections with images defined by sentences
\Phi_{1}(T),\Phi_{2}(T)
, the sentence

\exists X\,\exists Y:T=X+Y\wedge\Phi_{1}(X)\wedge\Phi_{2}(Y)

defines their sum by Proposition 27, and a similar trick works for multiplication. We may conclude

Corollary 28
.

(
[
8
]
,9) The set of constructible sections
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
forms a unital subring of
\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
.

Finally, we conclude with a proof that
\mathscr{O}_{X}(K)
is the ring of constructible and compatible sections
\sigma\in\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
. Since
\mathscr{O}_{X}
is already a collection of functions on
X
, we may conclude the constructible and compatible sections form a sheaf, as a treat.

Theorem 29
.

(
[
8
]
,9-10) For each (pro)constructible subspace
K\subseteq X
, the image of the map
i:\mathscr{O}_{X}(K)\to\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
is the ring of constructible and compatible sections.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\mathscr{O}_{X}(K)
, and suppose
\alpha,\beta\in K
with
\beta\in\overline{\alpha}
. Since
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is continuous in the weak and constructible topologies,
\sigma(\beta)
is a specialization of
\sigma(\alpha)
. I claim
\sigma(\beta)
is the unique specialization of
\sigma(\alpha)
in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
. If
\gamma\in\pi^{-1}(\beta)
is any specialization of
\sigma(\alpha)
, we know
\sigma(K)
is closed, which implies
\gamma\in\sigma(K)
. Consequently,

\gamma\in\sigma(K)\cap\pi^{-1}(\beta)=\{\sigma(\beta)\},

and
\sigma(\beta)
is the unique specialization of
\sigma(\alpha)
in
\pi^{-1}(\beta)
. By Theorem 25,
\sigma
is compatible, and
\sigma
is constructible by definition, so we have proved
i(\mathscr{O}_{X}(K))
is contained in the ring of constructible and compatible sections.

It is left to show that each constructible, compatible section is contained in the image of
i
. Let
\sigma\in\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
be a constructible and compatible section, which we can certainly view as a map
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
which sends
\alpha
to
\pi_{\alpha}(\sigma)\in\rho(\alpha)
. As before,
\sigma(K)
is constructible by definition. The morphism

\pi\biggr\lvert_{\sigma(K)}:\sigma(K)\to K

is bijective and continuous with respect to both the weak and constructible topologies, and with respect to the latter,
\pi\lvert_{\sigma(K)}
is a homeomorphism. Since
\sigma:K\to\sigma(K)
is the inverse of
\pi\lvert_{\sigma(K)}
,
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is continuous in the constructible topology. We still need to show that
\sigma(K)
is closed, and that
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is continuous in the weak topology.

Let
C
be a closed constructible subset of
\pi^{-1}(K)
. We know
\sigma^{-1}(C)
is constructible, and it is left to show
\sigma^{-1}(C)
is closed under specialization. Suppose
\alpha
is in
\sigma^{-1}(C)
with specialization
\beta
. Then
\sigma(\beta)\supseteq\sigma(\alpha)
, and since
C
is closed,
\sigma(\beta)\in C
. Hence
\beta\in\sigma^{-1}(C)
, implying
\sigma^{-1}(C)
is closed constructible and
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is continuous in the weak topology.

To show
\sigma(K)
is closed, it suffices by constructibility to show
\sigma(K)
is closed under specialization. Suppose
\alpha\in K
, and let
\gamma
be a specialization of
\sigma(\alpha)
. Since
\{\sigma(\alpha)\}
is closed in
\pi^{-1}(\alpha)
, if
\pi(\gamma)=\alpha
, then
\gamma=\sigma(\alpha)
. On the other hand, if
\pi(\gamma)\neq\alpha
, we still need to show
\gamma
is in the image of
\sigma
. Since
\gamma\supseteq\sigma(\alpha)
, we know
\pi(\gamma)\supseteq\alpha
, i.e.
\pi(\gamma)
is a specialization of
\alpha
. By Theorem 25,
\sigma(\pi(\gamma))
is the unique specialization of
\sigma(\alpha)
in
\pi^{-1}(\pi(\gamma))
, and so
\beta=\sigma(\pi(\beta))
and we may conclude this proof.
∎

From here onward, we will refer to
\mathscr{O}_{X}
as the sheaf of constructible and compatible sections on
X=\sper A
, and Theorem 29 shows us that we have two useful ways in which to think of this sheaf.

Definition 30
.

Let
X=\sper A
. The
real closure
of
A
is the ring
\mathscr{O}_{X}(X)
. For a (pro)constructible subset
K\subseteq X
, the
real closure of
A
on
K
is the ring
\mathscr{O}_{X}(K)
. A ring
A
is
real closed
if it is isomorphic to its real closure.

1.7.
Real Closed Rings are (co)Complete

We now turn towards the fundamental categorical properties of real closed rings, in an attempt to prove that the category of real closed rings with ring homomorphisms contains all limits and all colimits over small diagrams.

Definition 31
.

(
[
10
]
, Section 2, 35) Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S}
be two categories with
\mathcal{S}
a full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\iota:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}
be the inclusion functor. If
\iota
has a left adjoint
r:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}
, i.e. for all
A\in\text{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
and
B\in\text{Ob}(\mathcal{S})

\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\iota(B))=\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{S}}(r(A),B),

We call
r
a
reflector
, and
\mathcal{S}
a
reflective subcategory
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Being a reflective subcategory is a surprisingly strong condition;

Proposition 32
.

(
[
10
]
, Section 2, 38-39) Let
r:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}
be a reflector for the reflective subcategory
\mathcal{S}
. If
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete, then so is
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Left adjoints preserve colimits.
∎

We cannot, on the other hand, push limits from
\mathcal{C}
down to limits of
\mathcal{S}
, except for in special cases.

Proposition 33
.

(
[
10
]
,38-39) Let
r:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}
is a reflector such that for each object
A
in
\mathcal{C}
, each reflection
r_{A}:A\to r(A)
is an epimorphism. If
\mathcal{C}
is complete, then so is
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}:J\to\mathcal{C}
be a diagram with small index category
J
. Since
\mathcal{C}
is complete,

\lim_{j\in J}\mathcal{F}(j)

is in
\mathcal{C}
. Composing
r
with
\mathcal{F}
gives a diagram
r\mathcal{F}:J\to\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{S}
, and
\iota\circ r\mathcal{F}:J\to\mathcal{C}
also has a limit

\lim_{j\in J}r\mathcal{F}(j)

in
\mathcal{C}
. We will show that this limit lies in
\mathcal{S}
.

Showing

\lim_{j\in J}r\mathcal{F}(j)\in\mathcal{S}

is equivalent to showing that the map

\hat{r}:\lim_{j\in J}r\mathcal{F}(j)\to r\left(\lim_{j\in J}r\mathcal{F}(j)\right)

is an isomorphism. For ease, let

L:=\lim_{j\in J}r\mathcal{F}(j).

For each
j\in J
, we have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rL,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{r}}

and since
r
is an adjoint to the inclusion functor, we get a unique map
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rL}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

making the triangle commute. If
i\to j
is a map in
J
, the universal property of the limit
L
guarantees that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(j))}

commutes. We also have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rL}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(j)),}

but since we only know
rL
maps to
r(\mathcal{F}(i))
for all
i\in I
, and not that it respects the maps in the diagram
r\mathcal{F}
, we need to demonstrate this fact. The diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rL}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(j))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{r}}

commutes, and since
\hat{r}
is an epimorphism, the original triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rL}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(i))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(\mathcal{F}(j))}

commutes. We have now shown
rL
maps to the limit
L
of the diagram
r\mathcal{F}
, and by the universal property of the limit, this map is an isomorphism and
L
is in
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

We fix a notation for reflectors
r:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}
as in Proposition 32, and generalize the idea below:

Definition 34
.

A reflector
r:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}
is called an epireflector (respectively monoreflector) if
r_{A}:A\to rA
is an epimorphism (respectively monomorphism) for all
A\in\text{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proposition 35
.

All monoreflectors are epireflectors.

Proof.

Let
r:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}
be a monoreflector, and let
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}:rC\to D
be
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms such that
\varphi_{1}\circ r_{C}=\varphi_{2}\circ r_{C}
. Consider the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rD.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{i}\circ r_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{D}}

The morphism
rC\to rD
is
r_{D}\circ\varphi_{i}
by definition, but equals
r(\varphi_{i}\circ r_{C})
since
r
is a reflector. Hence

r_{D}\circ\varphi_{1}=r_{D}\circ\varphi_{2},

and since
r_{D}
is a monomorphism,
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}
.
∎

Let
POR/
\sqrt{0}
be the category of reduced partially ordered rings with ring homomorphisms. Let
WRR
be the full subcategory
\underline{\textbf{CRing}}
whose objects are rings
A
with the following property;

\forall a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A:\;\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{2}=0\right)\implies(a_{i}^{2}=0\;\forall i).

We call these rings
weakly real
(
[
10
]
,22-23). Using
\sper(-)
, we may construct a functor

\mathscr{F}:\textbf{WRR}\to\textbf{POR/$\sqrt{0}$}

as follows; given a weakly real ring
A
, the cone

P_{w}(A):=\sum A^{2}

guarantees that
\sper(A)\neq\emptyset
. So, the product of the canonical morphisms
\rho_{\alpha}
gives a morphism

A\to\prod_{\alpha\in\sper(A)}\rho(\alpha)

of partially ordered rings. Moving forward, we use the notation
\mathscr{F}(A)=\prod A
for

\prod_{\alpha\in\sper(A)}\rho(\alpha).

If
f:A\to B
is a ring homomorphism, I claim we obtain a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod(f)}

in rings.

We must first define the morphism
\mathscr{F}(f)=\prod(f)
. Let

\prod(f)((a_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\sper A})(\beta)=\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(a_{f^{-1}(\beta)}).

\prod(f)
is a well-defined map of sets because it is given in terms of set maps. If

(a_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\sper A},(b_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\sper A}\in\prod A,

we have

\displaystyle\prod(f)((a_{\alpha}+b_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\sper A})(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(a_{f^{-1}(\beta)}+b_{f^{-1}(\beta)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(a_{f^{-1}(\beta)})+\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(b_{f^{-1}(\beta)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod(f)(a_{\alpha})(\beta)+\prod(f)(b_{\alpha})(\beta).

A similar computation for multiplication shows that
\prod(f)
is a ring homomorphism.

Now, to show
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod(f)}

commutes, fix
a\in A
. Checking commutativity is equivalent to checking that

\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(a(f^{-1}(\beta)))=f(a)(\beta).

Restricting our focus to the point
\beta
, the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{f^{-1}(\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}

factors as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Both factor rings are integral domains, so we may factor our diagram through their ordered fields of fractions;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta).}

The universal property of real closed fields gives a unique map
\rho(f^{-1}(\beta))\to\rho(\beta)
such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A/\supp(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(f^{-1}(\beta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta)}

commutes, and so
\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}
must be that unique map!

Subrings of
\prod A
, the total ring of functions on
A
, play a crucial role in establishing the real closure operation as a reflector. We note that
\prod(-)
is not a reflector, as it is not generally idempotent (
[
10
]
, 55-56).

Let
\mathbb{R}_{alg}
be the field of real algebraic numbers, and let
\rho
be any real closed field. Since
\mathbb{Z}
is initial in the category of commutatve rings, there is a unique morphism
\mathbb{Z}\to\rho
. Since
\rho
is real closed, we have a factorization
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\sper(\mathbb{Z})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

Now,
\sper(\mathbb{F}_{p})=\emptyset
for characteristic reasons, so

\sper(\mathbb{Z})=\bigcup_{p\text{ prime}}\emptyset\cup\sper(\mathbb{Q})=\sper(\mathbb{Q}).

Since an ordered field structure on
\mathbb{Q}
must come from an ordering of
\mathbb{R}
, the real closure of
\mathbb{Q}
is
\mathbb{R}_{alg}
. So every real closed field
\rho
is a
\mathbb{R}_{alg}
algebra. We fittingly denote
\mathbb{R}_{alg}=\mathbb{R}_{0}
moving forward.

Let
\sigma(n)
denote the ring of semialgebraic functions in
n
variables. Given a semialgebraic function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{0}
, we may think of
\varphi
as an
n
-ary operation on
\mathbb{R}_{0}
. Furthermore, if
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}:\mathbb{R}_{0}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}_{0}
are semialgebraic, the operation

\omega_{\varphi}:\sigma(k)^{n}\to\sigma(k)

which maps
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\mapsto\varphi(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})

is an
n
-ary operation on
\sigma(k)
for every natural
k
. We also know a set map
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{0}
is semialgebraic if and only if
\varphi(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})
is semialgebraic for all
n
-tuples of semialgebraic functions
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})
. Hence semialgebraic functions correspond bijectively to
n
-ary operations on semialgebraic functions.

Given a semialgebraic function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{0}
and any total ring of functions
\prod A
, we obtain an
n
-ary operation on
\prod A
as follows; since

\prod A=\prod_{\alpha\in\sper(A)}\rho(\alpha),

and
\rho(\alpha)
is a
\mathbb{R}_{0}
-algebra, we may form the tensor product

\varphi\otimes_{\mathbb{R}_{0}}1_{A}:\mathbb{R}_{0}^{n}\otimes\prod A\to\mathbb{R}_{0}\otimes\prod A.

The multiplication operation in
\prod A
allows us to write the map as

\varphi_{A}:\left(\prod A\right)^{n}\to\prod A,

so
\varphi
also gives an
n
-ary operation on total rings of functions.

We have a notion of ’
n
-ary operations on
A
’ (
[
10
]
,65) which does not align with the usual use of those words. Let

\Delta_{A}:A\to\prod A

be the canonical map from
A
to its total ring of functions. Let
\omega
be an
n
-ary operation on
\prod A
. The composition
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\prod_{\alpha\in\sper A}\rho(\alpha)\right)^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{\alpha\in\sper A}\rho(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{A}^{\times n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}

is a map of
A
-algebras such that

\omega(\Delta_{A}^{\times n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))(\beta)=\omega(a_{1}(\beta),\ldots,a_{n}(\beta)).

Definition 36
.

Let
A
be a weakly real ring. An element
f\in\prod A
is a
semialgebraic function on
A
if there exists a natural
n
,
\{a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\}\subseteq A
and an
n
-ary operation
\omega
such that

f(\alpha)=\omega(\Delta_{A}^{\times n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))(\alpha)

for all
\alpha\in\sper A
.

We denote the semialgebraic functions on A by
\semf(A)
.
n
-ary operations are well-behaved under ring homomorphisms;

Proposition 37
.

(
[
10
]
,66) If
f:A\to B
is a ring homomorphism of weakly real rings, and
\omega
is an
n
-ary operation, then
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod(f)}

commutes in
Sets
.

Proof.

Indeed, for each
\beta\in\sper B
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A
we have

\displaystyle\prod(f)(\omega(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}))(\beta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(\omega(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})(f^{-1}(\beta)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(\omega_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(a_{1}(f^{-1}(\beta)),\ldots,a_{n}(f^{-1}(\beta)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(a_{1}(f^{-1}(\beta))),\ldots,\iota_{f^{-1}(\beta)}(a_{n}(f^{-1}(\beta))))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(f(a_{1})(\beta),\ldots,f(a_{n})(\beta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(f(a_{1}),\ldots,f(a_{n}))(\beta).

∎

In the above proof, we may note that the semialgebraic functions
\omega(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
are mapped to semialgebraic functions
\omega(f(a_{1}),\ldots,f(a_{n}))
by
\prod(f)
, for any ring homomorphism
f:A\to B
. Hence
f
gives a map
\semf(f):\semf(A)\to\semf(B)
.

Semialgebraic functions are also preserved under
n
-ary operations;

Proposition 38
.

(
[
10
]
,67) Suppose
A
is a weakly real ring,
\omega:\left(\prod A\right)^{n}\to\prod A
is an
n
-ary operation, and
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{i},\ldots,\omega_{n}
are semialgebraic functions on
A
parameterized by
(a_{i,j})_{1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq j\leq r_{j}}
. Then

\omega(\omega_{1}(a_{1,1},\ldots,a_{1,n_{1}}),\ldots,\omega_{n}(a_{n,1},\ldots,a_{n,r_{n}}))

is semialgebraic.

Proof.

Let
\Phi(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y)
be a first-order formula describing the graph of
\omega
, and let
\Phi_{i}(X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,n_{i}},X_{i})
be a first order sentence describing the graph of
\omega_{i}
. Let
\Psi(X_{1,1},\ldots,X_{n,r_{n}},Y)
be the sentence

\exists X_{1,1},\ldots,X_{n,r_{n}}:\left(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i}(X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,n_{i}},X_{i})\right)\wedge\Phi(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},Y).

The sentence
\Psi
is a first order sentence which also describes the graph of a semialgebraic function; in fact, it describes

\omega(\omega_{1}(a_{1,1},\ldots,a_{1,n_{1}}),\ldots,\omega_{n}(a_{n,1},\ldots,a_{n,r_{n}})),

concluding this proof
∎

In fact, the
\text{semf}(\cdot)
operation is idempotent, i.e. the natural map
\semf(A)\to\semf(\semf(A))
is an isomorphism (
[
10
]
,75). More strongly, it can be shown that
\semf:\textbf{POR/$\sqrt{0}$}\to\textbf{\lx@text@underline{semf}}
is a monoreflector. The fact that
A\to\semf(A)
is a monomorphism follows from the factorization
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\semf(A),}

where the top morphism is the canonical map from
A
to its total ring of functions
\prod A
. To show
\semf(\cdot)
is a left adjoint of the forgetful functor, by (
[
10
]
,71-72), if
A
is a reduced weakly real ring, the map

A\to\semf(A)

is an epimorphism in
POR/
\sqrt{0}
.

Theorem 39
.

(
[
10
]
,89) Semialgebraic functions are the smallest monoreflective subcategory of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
.

Proof.

To prove this theorem, we will first prove a key lemma;

Lemma 40
.

If
r:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
is a fixed monoreflector and
E
is an epicomplete object, then

r_{E}:E\to rE

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Suppose
r_{E}:E\to rE
is an epimorphism and a monomorphism, with
E
an object of
\mathscr{D}
. Since
r
is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor, we have a morphism

\eta_{E}:rE\to E

corresponding to the identity morphism
E\to E
via adjunction. Consider the sequence of morphisms
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{E}}

We note that
r_{E}
is induced by the identity, and since
r_{E}
is an epimorphism, any other morphism
rE\to E
must be induced by the identity. Hence

\eta_{E}\circ r_{E}=\text{id}_{E}.

Now for
rE
, we have a corresponding diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rE}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces rE.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{E}}

The correspondence of morphisms under adjunction is bijective, and since
r_{E}
corresponds to
both

\text{id}_{rE}
and
r_{E}\circ\eta_{E}
,

\text{id}_{rE}=r_{E}\circ\eta_{E}

and
r_{E}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Hence every monoreflective subcategory of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
must contain the epicomplete objects in
POR/
\sqrt{0}
. By (
[
10
]
,76), Corollary 7.20,
semf
equals the epicomplete objects in
POR/
\sqrt{0}
, proving the theorem we set out to show.
∎

Semialgebraic functions form a full subcategory of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
, and if we restrict further to continuous semialgebraic functions, we get another full subcategory of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
. We can show that both of these subcategories are reflective subcategories of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
. The easiest way to give the reflector is by first introducing the definition of
H-closed
reflectors, which allows us define reflectors solely by specifying what they do on the reduced partially ordered rings
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. We will conclude by showing that
\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
maps via epimorphism to its real closure, meaning the real closure is an H-closed monoreflector of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
.

Definition 41
.

A full isomorphism closed subcategory
\mathscr{D}
of a concrete category
\mathscr{C}
is
H-closed
if for all
X\in\text{ob}(\mathscr{D})
and for all
Y\in\text{ob}(\mathscr{C})
with a surjection
X\twoheadrightarrow Y
,
Y\in\text{ob}(\mathscr{D})
.

Some examples and non-examples of H-closed reflectors are given on pages 119-123 of
[
10
]
, though we focus on the real closure reflector moving forward. Let

\rho:\textbf{POR/$\sqrt{0}$}\to\textbf{RCR}

be the functor between full subcategories of
CRing
which takes a reduced partially ordered ring (with non-empty real spectrum)
A
to the ring of continuous semialgebraic functions
\rho A
on
A
. To verify this is a functor, we need an assignment of morphisms which respects composition and identity. If
f\in\rho A
and
\varphi:A\to B
is a ring homomorphism, let

f=\omega_{f}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}),

where
\omega_{f}
is an
n
-ary operation and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A
. By Proposition 34,

\varphi(f)=\omega_{f}(\varphi(a_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(a_{n})),

and since
\omega_{f}
is an
n
-ary operation corresponding to a continuous semialgebraic function, the function
\varphi(f)
is continuous and semialgebraic. Since the total ring of functions is a functor whose source category is any subcategory of weakly real rings, it preserves composition and identity, and consequently so does
\rho
.

To show
\rho
is a reflector is equivalent to showing every map
\varphi
from a ring
A
to a real closed ring
B
factors uniquely through the real closure of
A
. The proof is to simply show
\rho
is a left adjoint to the forgetful functor via commutativity of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\varphi)}

so we omit the details. Since we have a factorization

A\to\rho A\to\semf(A)

and
\underline{\textbf{semf}}
is monoreflective,
A\to\rho A
is a monomorphism and
RCR
is a monoreflective subcategory of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
.

In fact, we can
\rho
is an H-closed reflector of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
. By (
[
10
]
,114-115), Theorem 10.6, H-closed reflectors of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
are uniquely determined by how they reflect the partially ordered reduced rings

\{\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}},

with each ordered by its ’sums of squares’ positive cone.

Proposition 42
.

Let
\mathscr{F}(n):=\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, together with the partial order given by sums of squares. Then the map
