\rho_{\mathscr{F}(n)}:\mathscr{F}(n)\to\rho\mathscr{F}(n)

is an epimorphism.

Proof.

The central observation in this proof is that
\mathscr{F}(n)
is
already
a collection of continuous
n
-ary operations on any real closed field! Let
C\in\text{ob}(\textbf{POR/$\sqrt{0}$})
, and let

\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}:\rho\mathscr{F}(n)\to C

be two partially ordered ring homomorphisms such that

\varphi_{1}\circ\rho_{\mathscr{F}(n)}=\varphi_{2}\circ\rho_{\mathscr{F}(n)}.

In particular, the morphisms
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
map the variables
x_{i}
to the same elements of
C
. Let
\omega(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})
be an arbitrary element of
\rho\mathscr{F}(n)
with each
a_{j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathscr{F}(n)
. We then have

\displaystyle(\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2})\omega(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\omega(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})-\varphi_{2}\omega(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(\varphi_{1}(a_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})),\ldots,\varphi_{1}(a_{m}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})))-

\displaystyle\omega(\varphi_{2}(a_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})),\ldots,\varphi_{2}(a_{m}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(a_{1}(\varphi_{1}(x_{1}),\ldots,\varphi_{1}(x_{n})),\ldots,a_{m}(\varphi_{1}(x_{1}),\ldots,\varphi_{1}(x_{n})))-

\displaystyle\omega(a_{1}(\varphi_{2}(x_{1}),\ldots,\varphi_{2}(x_{n})),\ldots,a_{m}(\varphi_{2}(x_{1}),\ldots,\varphi_{2}(x_{n})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

proving that
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}
as
\mathscr{F}(n)
-module homomorphisms, and since they are ring maps, we’ve proved that
\rho_{\mathscr{F}(n)}
is an epimorphism.
∎

Proposition 43
.

If
R
is a real closed ring and
\varphi:R\to S
is a surjective ring homomorphism, then
S
is real closed.

Proof.

Since
\rho:\textbf{POR/$\sqrt{0}$}\to\textbf{RCR}
is a reflector, we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\varphi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\rho}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\varphi)}

in the category of
R
-modules, where
K_{\rho}
is the kernel of
\rho(\varphi)
. We know
\rho_{R}
is an isomorphism, so we may view
\ker(\varphi)
as an ideal of
\rho R
. If we can show

\rho_{R}\biggr\lvert_{\ker(\varphi)}:\ker(\varphi)\to K_{\rho}

is an isomorphism of
R
-modules, the Snake Lemma will show
\rho_{S}
is an isomorphism, and the proof will be complete.

We know
\rho_{R}|_{\ker(\varphi)}
is an injective homomorphism because it is a composition of injective morphisms. It is left to show surjectivity of
\rho_{R}|_{\ker(\varphi)}
. Let
\omega(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})
be an element of
K_{\rho}
. We may consider
\omega(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})
as an element
r\in R
. We then have

\displaystyle\varphi(r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(\rho_{R}^{-1}(\omega(r_{1},\ldots,r_{k})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(\omega(\rho_{R}^{-1}(r_{1}),\ldots,\rho_{R}^{-1}(r_{k})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(\varphi\circ\rho_{R}^{-1}(r_{1}),\ldots,\varphi\circ\rho_{R}^{-1}(r_{k}))

is in
\rho S
since
\varphi\circ\rho_{R}^{-1}(r_{i})
is in
S
, so by commutativity, we have

\omega(\varphi\circ\rho_{R}^{-1}(a_{1}),\ldots,\varphi\circ\rho_{R}^{-1}(a_{k}))=0.

Hence
r\in\ker(\varphi)
, implying
\rho_{R}|_{\ker(\varphi)}
is an isomorphism of
R
-modules, and the Snake Lemma says
\rho_{S}
is an
R
-module isomorphism as well.
∎

We have now shown
\rho:\textbf{POR/$\sqrt{0}$}\to\textbf{RCR}
is an H-closed monoreflector of
POR/
\sqrt{0}
, so Propositions 31, 32, and 34 together give that
RCR
is complete and co-complete.

2.
Properties of Real Closed Rings

We abbreviate
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(A)
as
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}A
. Since real closed rings are sections of the structure sheaf on
\sper A
, the following algebraic proofs use the geometry of
\sper A
and the theory built up in the first section.

Proposition 44
.

(
[
8
]
, 12) Let
K
be a (pro)constructible subspace of
X=\sper(A)
. If
\sigma\in\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}K)
and
\sigma(\alpha)\neq 0
in
\rho(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in K
, then
\sigma\in\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}K)^{*}
.

Proof.

Define
\tau\in\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
such that

t(\alpha):=\frac{1}{\sigma(\alpha)}.

Since
\sigma:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is compatible and inverses are preserved under ring homomorphisms,
\tau:K\to\pi^{-1}(K)
is also compatible. It is left to show
\tau
is constructible.

Let
\Phi(T)
be a sentence in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}A
such that
\Phi(\sigma(\alpha))
and

\exists x:\Phi(x)\wedge\,\forall\;y(\Phi(y)\implies y=x)

holds for all
\alpha\in K
. Let
\Psi(T)
be the sentence

\exists T:1=Tx\wedge\Phi(x).

We certainly have
\Psi(\tau(\alpha))
by definition of
\tau
. Additionally, in each
\rho(\alpha)
, there is an element
\tau(\alpha)
such that
\Psi(\tau(\alpha))
. If there is an element
\omega\in\rho(\alpha)
such that
\Psi(\omega)
, since
\sigma(\alpha)
is the unique element satisfying
\Phi(T)
in
\rho(\alpha)
,

\omega\sigma(\alpha)=1,

which implies

\omega=\frac{1}{\sigma(\alpha)}=\tau(\alpha).

By Proposition 27,
\tau
is constructible, and so
\tau
is a constructible and compatible inverse to
\sigma
in
\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}K)
.
∎

Proposition 45
.

(
[
8
]
, 12) Let
\sigma\in\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}K)
. If
\sigma(\alpha)\geq 0
for all
\alpha\in K
, there is a section
\tau\in\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}K)
such that

•

\tau(\alpha)\geq 0
for all
\alpha\in K
, and

•

\sigma=\tau^{2}
.

Proof.

For all
\alpha\in K
, define

\tau(\alpha):=+\sqrt{\sigma(\alpha)}.

Since the polynomial
x^{2}-\sigma(\alpha)
has exactly two solutions in
\rho(\alpha)
, one of which is positive,
+\sqrt{\sigma(\alpha)}
is the unique positive root of
\sigma(\alpha)
. So
\tau
is well-defined, and a consequence of being defined by
\sigma
is that
\tau
is compatible. It is left to show
\tau
is a constructible section.

Let Let
\Phi(T)
be a sentence in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}A
such that
\Phi(\sigma(\alpha))
and

\exists x:\Phi(x)\wedge\,\forall\;y(\Phi(y)\implies y=x)

holds for all
\alpha\in K
. Let
\Psi
be the sentence

\exists T:T\geq 0\wedge\Phi(T^{2}).

We then know
\Psi(\tau(\alpha))
for each
\alpha
, and
\tau(\alpha)
is unique because
\sigma(\alpha)
has exactly one positive root. We have now proved the claim.
∎

Unlike the category of schemes, we can extend all the sections in our structure sheaf to the whole space:

Theorem 46
.

(
[
8
]
, 14) Let
L\subseteq K
be a constructible subset of a (pro)constructible subspace
K\subseteq\sper A
. Let
\sigma\in\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
be a compatible section such that

\sigma\biggr\lvert_{L}\in\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}L),

and
\sigma(\alpha)=0
for all
\alpha\in K\setminus L
. Then
\sigma\in\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}K)
.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
\sigma\in\prod_{\alpha\in K}\rho(\alpha)
is a compatible section, so if we can show
\sigma
is a constructible section, we’ll have our proof. Let
\Phi_{L}\in\mathscr{L}_{\leq}A
be a sentence defining
L\subseteq K
. We know
\sigma\lvert_{L}
is constructible by hypothesis, so by Proposition 27, there is a sentence
\Phi_{\sigma}(T)
in
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}A
with free variable
T
such that
\sigma(\alpha)
is the unique element of
\rho(\alpha)
satisfying
\Phi_{\sigma}(T)
for every
\alpha\in L
. The formula

(\neg\Phi_{L}\wedge T=0)\bigvee(\Phi_{L}\wedge\Phi_{\sigma}(T))

defines
\sigma
, so
\sigma\in\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}K)
.
∎

Real Closed Spaces I

3.2.
Subspaces

Given a real closed space
X
, a topological subspace
S
of
X
which is subspace in the weak topology on
X
can be given a structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}_{S}
so that
(S,\mathscr{O}_{S})
is a locally ringed space. Depending on how the topological subspace
S
sits in the topology of
X
, we have different ways of speaking about subspaces, so allow me to elucidate this point below.

Definition 51
.

Let
X
be a real closed space, and let
U
be a topological subspace of
X
which is open in
X
. Then the space
U
together with the structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}_{U}:=\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}{}_{U}
is an
open subspace
of
X
.

Example 52
.

Say
X=\sper\mathbb{R}[x,y]
, and in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}\mathbb{R}[x,y]
, we consider the sentence
\Phi_{U}

\exists X,Y\text{ s.t. }\neg(X=0)\bigvee\neg(Y=0),

and let
U
be the subspace of
X
define by
\Phi_{U}
. If
\beta\in U
and
\alpha
is a generization of
\beta
, we have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}[x,y]/\supp(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}[x,y]/\supp(\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\beta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\beta}}

Since
\rho(\beta)\models\Phi_{U}
, we know one at least one of
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
is not in
\ker(\rho_{\beta})
, and Without loss of generality, we assume
\bar{x}\notin\ker(\rho_{\beta})
. Since
\mathbb{R}[x,y]/\supp(\alpha)\to\mathbb{R}[x,y]/\supp(\beta)
is surjective,
\bar{x}
has a nonzero element
x+\supp(\alpha)
in its preimage. Consequently, the composite map

\mathbb{R}[x,y]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{R}[x,y]/\supp(\alpha)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}(\alpha)

does not have
x
in its kernel, proving
\rho_{\alpha}\models\Phi_{U}
Hence
U
is closed under generization, proving
U
is open. It is left to describe the relationship between the sheaves
\mathscr{O}_{\rcspace}and
O
_U

_U

$$\exists X,Y\text{ s.t. }X^{2}+Y^{2}<\frac{4}{n}.$$Each $\Phi_{n}$ defines an open subspace $U_{n}\subset X$, and let
$$W=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}.$$We certainly have the homomorphism $\mathbb{R}[x,y]\to\mathbb{R}$ given by $x,y\mapsto 0$ satisfies $\Phi_{n}$ for all natural $n$, so $W$ contains at least one closed point. Taking the transcendence degree 2 extension $\mathbb{R}(x,y)$ of $\mathbb{R}$, we can take the real closure of $\mathbb{R}(x,y)$ with respect to any ordering. For example, the set
$$\gamma:=\left\{f(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}(x,y):\exists\epsilon>0\text{ such that }f\biggr\lvert_{[0,2\epsilon]\times[0,\epsilon]}\geq 0\right\}$$orders $\mathbb{R}(x,y)$, and $\mathbb{R}[x,y]\to\mathbb{R}(\gamma)$ satisfies $\Phi_{n}$ with $2Y=X=\epsilon$, and so $\gamma\in W$.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\end{center}While we cannot always lift the morphism between the valuative space and $Y$ to a morphism between the valuative space and $X$, we can in this example. We know the set-theoretic image of $f$ is the subspace
$$\{\mathbb{R}_{0}[u,v]\to\rho:v>_{\rho}0\},$$and we may define a morphism $g:\im(f)\to X$ given by the homomorphism $$\Gamma(\sper\left(\mathbb{R}_{0}[x,y,z]/\langle z-y^{2}\rangle)_{y}\right)\to\Gamma(\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[u,v])$$determined by $x\mapsto u,z\mapsto v$, and $y$ is mapped to the unique positive square root of $v$. Since $X$ may be written as the disjoint union of $X\cap(y>0)$ and $X\cap(y<0)$, we see $\im(g)=X\cap(y>0)$, and $f\circ g=\text{id}_{\im(f)}$. We then have a section by composing the local section $g$ with $j$;
\begin{center}\hbox to133.4pt{\vbox to53.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 66.69675pt\lower-27.64441pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

3.3.
Morphisms

The last example of the previous section begs the question, is the image of a morphism always a subspace? While the answer is no generally, the answer is yes if the morphism is quasicompact.

Definition 58
.

(
[
8
]
,56) A morphism
f:X\to Y
of real closed spaces is
quasicompact
if for all open affine
\sper B\subseteq Y
,
f^{-1}(\sper B)
is a quasicompact subspace of X. More explicitly, any open cover
\{\sper A_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of
f^{-1}(\sper B)
has a finite subcover
\sper A_{1},\ldots,\sper A_{n}
.

Proposition 59
.

(
[
8
]
) If
f:X\to Y
is a quasicompact morphism of real closed spaces and
K
is a constructible subspace of
X
, then
f(K)
is locally constructible in
Y
.

Proof.

We want to prove, in any open
\sper B
of
Y
containing a point in the image , that
f(K)\cap\sper B
is constructible. Since
f
is quasicompact, we can write

f^{-1}(\sper B)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i}

and for a single open affine
\sper A_{j}
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\cap\sper A_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\lvert_{\sper A_{j}}}

commutes. By Theorem 18
[
2
]
, the image of
K\cap\sper A_{j}
in
\sper B
is constructible, and so the finite union

\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}f(K\cap\sper A_{i})=f(K)\cap\sper B

is constructible as well.
∎

If
X=\sper A
and
Y=\sper B
, for any basic open
D(g)\subseteq Y
, the limit over the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B}

is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\left(f^{\#}(g)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\lvert_{D\left(f^{\#}(g)\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

so every basic open set has basic inverse image, which is itself open and affine. We have now proved

Proposition 60
.

(
[
8
]
,58) Morphisms of affine real closed spaces are quasicompact.

Luckily, quasicompact morphisms are stable under base change, just as they are in the category of schemes:

Proposition 61
.

(
[
8
]
,59) If
f:X\to Y
is a quasicompact morphism, and
t:T\to Y
is any morphism, we may form the fiber product
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

If
f
is quasicompact, then so is
p_{T}
.

Proof.

We will proceed to show
p_{T}
is quasicompact by showing that
T
has an open cover, and when we restrict the codomain to each affine in the cover, we obtain a quasicompact morphism. Choose an open affine
\sper B
of
Y
, and base change to obtain the fiber square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}^{-1}(f^{-1}(\sper B))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}(\sper B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(\sper B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B.}

since
f
is quasicompact, we rewrite

f^{-1}(\sper B)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i},

which allows us to rewrite our diagram as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(\sper B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B.}

Choose an open affine cover
\{\sper R_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of
t^{-1}(\sper B)
. For a fixed
\lambda
, we may take the fiber product over
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}^{-1}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper R_{\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(\sper B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B,}

which is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper(R_{\lambda}\otimes_{B}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}^{-1}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper R_{\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(\sper B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B.}

Since
\sper B
is arbitrary and each point of
T
is contained in some
t^{-1}(\sper B)
, we have shown that

p_{X}^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\sper A_{i}\right)\to t^{-1}(\sper B)

is quasicompact independent of
B
, implying that
p_{T}
is quasicompact as well.
∎

Quasiseparated morphisms are similar;

Definition 62
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism, and consider the fibered square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

The pair of identity morphisms to
X
give a morphism

\Delta_{f}:X\to X\times_{Y}X

called the diagonal morphism of
f
. We say
f
is
quasiseparated
is
\Delta_{f}
is quasicompact.

Proposition 63
.

(
[
8
]
, 59) Let
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

be morphisms of real closed spaces such that
g\circ f
is quasicompact and
g
is quasiseparated. Then
f
is quasiseparated.

Proof.

This proof is entirely an application of base change. We have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}

with limit
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}

Since quasicompactness is stable under base change,
p_{Y}
is quasicompact. We also have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{Z}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\text{id}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}

with limit
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{Z}Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\text{id}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}

By the properties of fiber products,
X\times_{Y}Y\cong X
, and the induced morphism from
X
to
X\times_{Z}Y
by composing
p_{2}
with this isomorphism is the graph
\Gamma_{f}
of
f
. Since
\Delta_{g}
is quasicompact by assumption,
\Gamma_{f}
is quasicompact by base change. Since compositions of quasicompact morphisms are quasicompact,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}

is quasicompact and equals
f
, so the claim is proved.
∎

Quasiseparated morphisms, just like quasicompact morphisms, are closed under composition and base change.

Proposition 64
.

(
[
8
]
,61) Quasiseparated morphisms are closed under base change; that is, if
f:X\to Y
is quasiseparated and
T\to Y
is any morphism, then
f_{T}:X_{T}\to T
is quasiseparated, where

X_{T}=X\times_{Y}T
.

Proof.

We aim to show that the diagonal
X_{T}\to X_{T}\times_{T}X_{T}
is quasicompact. First, by associativity of fiber products,

X_{T}\times_{T}X_{T}=\left(X\times_{Y}T\right)\times_{T}\left(X\times_{Y}T\right)\cong X\times_{Y}\left(T\times_{T}X\right)\times_{Y}T\cong X\times_{Y}X\times_{Y}T.

Consider the fiber product
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X\times_{Y}T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}

which is isomorphic to
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}\times_{T}X_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

via the unique diagram isomorphism from associativity. If we then map into this diagram using the diagonal
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}\times_{T}X_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}

we see the fiber product is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{X}\left(X\times_{Y}T\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}\times_{T}X_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}

by the magic square diagram, which is uniquely isomorphic to
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{T}\times_{T}X_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{f}}

Since
X_{T}\to X_{T}\times_{T}X_{T}
is the base change of a quasicompact morphism, it is quasicompact, so
f_{T}
is quasiseparated.
∎

Proposition 65
.

(
[
8
]
,61) In the composition
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

if
f
and
g
are quasiseparated, then so is
g\circ f
.

Proof.

We aim to show
\Delta_{g\circ f}:X\to X\times_{Z}X
is quasicompact. By the universal property of fiber products, we have a morphism

X\times_{Z}X\to Y\times_{Z}Y

induced by
f\times f
. We then have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{Z}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}

and the fiber product is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{Z}Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times f}

The morphism
X\times_{Y}X\to X\times_{Z}X
is the base change of
\Delta_{g}
, so it is quasicompact. We map
X
to this square using
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{Z}Y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times f}

and the unique induced morphism is in fact
\Delta_{f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{Z}Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times f}

We have now demonstrated
\Delta_{g\circ f}=(f\times f)\circ\Delta_{f}
, and since a composition of quasicompact morphisms is quasicompact, we are done.
∎

While the proofs are slightly different in some cases, pages 58-64 of
[
8
]
give theorems on the intersections of quasicompact open sets in quasiseparated real closed spaces, in addition to some generalizations of theorems which hold for schemes to locally proconstructible spaces. All of which is to say, while the topology of real closed spaces is quite different from (and much finer than) that of schemes, the categorical properties are quite similar. Since quasiseparated, separated, quasicompact, and finite type morphisms are all morphism properties given in terms of diagrams, they look very similar in the scheme and real closed category. The situation is in sharp contrast with the definition of a regular morphism (
[
8
]
,67), which is unrelated to the notion of a regular local ring.

3.4.
A Deep Example: Semialgebraic Geometry & Real Closed Spaces

Some of the best behaved real closed spaces come from familiar classical geometric objects.

Let
k
be a real closed field, and let
\Phi(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
be a sentence in
n
variables in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(k)
. We may consider the sentence
\Phi
in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}])
, and by Section 1.5, there is an associated real closed space
K_{\Phi}\hookrightarrow\sper k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
. The closed points of
K_{\Phi}
correspond to
k
-algebra homomorphisms
k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]\to k
, or maximal convex ideals
\textbf{p}\in\text{Spec }k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
. All maximal convex ideals of
k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
are ideals of the form
\langle t_{1}-a_{1},\ldots,t_{n}-a_{n}\rangle
for
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in k^{n}
. Hence the closed points of
K_{\Phi}
correspond to the points in the real vector space
k^{n}
.

We can say more about real closed spaces
K_{\Phi}\hookrightarrow\sper k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
. Suppose
g(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})>0
is an atomic formula in
\Phi
, written in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}])
. For any closed point
x\in K_{\Phi}
, the kernel
p
of
x:k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]\to k
is given by a vector
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in k^{n}
. By definition,

x\models g(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})>0,

and rephrasing for ease of understanding,

g(x(t_{1}),\ldots,x(t_{n}))>0

with
x(t_{i})\in k
. We then know
x(t_{i}-a_{i})=x(t_{i})-a_{i}=0
, so

g(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})>0,

and each atomic formula gives a polynomial (in)equality describing a region of
k^{n}
. Since the atomic formula here can be replaced with any other atomic formula in
\Phi
, the real closed space
K_{\Phi}
corresponds to the semialgebraic space defined by
\Phi
in
k^{n}
.

Let’s refer to real closed spaces like
K_{\Phi}
as
semialgebraic spaces
. Semialgebaic spaces are quasicompact and quasiseparated, and it is not difficult to prove. Perhaps the most important part about these spaces is that the ambiguity present in deciding which real closed field a subspace should be defined over evaporates.

If
X
is a real closed space, a curve in
X
should be a ’one dimensional’ subspace
C\hookrightarrow X
, but since
X
may not be equidimensional, defining a curve this way is annoying. Instead, we prefer to think of a curve as a morphism

\gamma:[0,1]\to X,

but what exactly do we mean by
[0,1]
? We may define
[0,1]
over
\mathbb{R}_{0}
using the sentence

\exists t:0\leq t\leq 1

in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R}_{0}[t])
. Since we may replace
\mathbb{R}_{0}
with any ring here, which ring should we choose to define our interval over?

With semialgebraic spaces, the choice is obvious. Suppose
K_{\Phi}\hookrightarrow\sper k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
is a semialgebraic space. Define
[0,1]_{k}
using the sentence

\exists t:0\leq t\leq 1

in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(k[t])
. If
C\subset K_{\Phi}
is a subspace of dimension one, fix a closed point
x\in C\subset K_{\Phi}
, then let
W_{x}^{C}
be the local subspace of
x
in the curve
C
. We then have a commutative triangle describing
C
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle[0,1]_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{\Phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper(k),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

where
\gamma
factors through
C
. If the closed point
x
is in the image of
\gamma
, there is a number
z\in k
between 0 and 1 such that

\gamma\lvert_{W_{z}}:W_{z}\to W_{x}^{C}

is an isomorphism. Such a morphism
\gamma
is like a local parameterization of
C
in a neighborhood of
x
.

3.5.
The Real Étale Site

Now, to form a site from the category of real closed spaces, we need some notion of étale morphism for real closed spaces. To begin, we introduce finite type morphisms in the real closed category. This choice allows us strong control over the images of quasicompact morphisms of real closed spaces, while also making the model theory of real closed spaces an effective tool in translating between algebra (i.e. sheaves) and geometry of these spaces.

Definition 66
.

A morphism
f:X\to Y
is
locally of finite type
if for each point
x\in X
, there are open affine subspaces
V
containing
f(x)
and
U
containing
x
such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\lvert_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{U,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V}}

commutes, where
\pi_{V}
is projection from the fiber product over
\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}
, and
i_{U,V}
is a monomorphism.
f
is
finite type
if it is locally of finite type and quasicompact.

Remark 67
.

If
U\cong\sper A
and
V\cong\sper B
are affine, assume
A
and
B
are real closed. Since
B
is an algebra over
\mathbb{R}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]=

\displaystyle\sper B\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\sper B\otimes_{\mathbb{R}_{0}}\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\sper B[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}].

Although the ring
B[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
is not real closed, it is convenient to write its real spectrum as
\sper B[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
to define
U
as a constuctible subset, since we’d like to use the variables
t_{i}
to write the sentence
\Phi_{U}
which defines
U
in
V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
.

Remark 68
.

If
f:X\to Y
is finite type, consider the factorization
X\to f(X)\to Y
of
f
. Let
V
be a nonempty open in
Y
, and choose open affine
U
such that we have a commutative triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\lvert_{U}}

We then have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V,}

which yields a morphism
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)\times_{Y}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V.}

We consequently know
U
maps to
V
in two ways;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)\times_{Y}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V.}

We may then map to the fiber product
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)\times_{Y}V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)\times_{Y}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V,}

and since the morphism
U\to V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
is a monomorphism,
U\to f(X)\times_{Y}V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
is as well. We know
f(X)\times_{Y}V\cong f(X)\cap V
, so in conclusion
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(X)\cap V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f(X)\cap V)\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V}\lvert_{f(X)}}

commutes, and
X\to f(X)
is a finite type surjection. We may thus check whether a morphism is finite type by checking if it is finite type onto its image!

The previous remark essentially proves that if
X\to Y\to Z
is locally of finite type, so is
X\to Y

Proposition 69
.

(
[
8
]
,90-91) If the composition
g\circ f
of the morphisms
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
is locally of finite type, then so is
f

Proof.

Analogously to the argument above, an open
U
of
X
maps to
Y\times_{Z}V
for some open
V\subseteq Z
, and this factors through
Y\times_{Z}V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
via a monomorphism followed by a projection.
∎

Proposition 70
.

(
[
8
]
,90-91) If
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are locally of finite type, then so is
g\circ f
.

Proof.

If
g
is locally finite type, there is a natural
m\in\mathbb{N}
and an open
V\subseteq Y
mapping to a nonempty open
W\subseteq Z
such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\lvert_{V}}

commutes. If
f
is locally of finite type, there is a natural
n
, an open
U\subseteq X
and open
V\subseteq Y
(shrink
V
here if necessary) such that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\lvert_{U}}

commutes. Taking the product of
V\hookrightarrow W\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}]
with
\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]
yields a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n+m}].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V}}

We consequently have a commutative triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n+m}],}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g\circ f)\lvert_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{W}}

concluding this proof.
∎

Proposition 71
.

(
[
8
]
,90-91) If
f:X\to Y
is locally of finite type and
t:T\to Y
is an arbitrary morphism,
f_{T}:X_{T}\to T
is locally of finite type.

Proof.

The idea here is since ’locally of finite type’ is a property given in terms of commutative diagrams, base changing the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{V}}

over
Y
by the morphism
t
gives the desired commutative triangle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(V)\times\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{t^{-1}(V)}}

I leave it to the reader to check the relevant fiber product isomorphisms.
∎

Definition 72
.

A morphism
f:X\to Y
of real closed spaces is
real étale
if
f
is a quasicompact and quasiseparated morphism, and a local isomorphism which is locally of finite type.

As with the usual definition of étale, we need real étale morphisms to be closed under composition and base change, as well as have left cancellation.

Proposition 73
.

Let
f:X\to Y
,
g:Y\to Z
and
t:T\to Y
be morphisms of real closed spaces. Then

(1)

If
f\,\text{and }\,g
are real étale, then so is
g\circ f

(2)

If
f
is real étale and
t
is arbitrary, then the induced morphism
X\times_{Y}T\to T
is real étale

(3)

If
g\circ f
and
g
are both real étale, then so is
f
.

Proof.

Since quasicompact morphisms, quasiseparated morphisms, locally finite type morphisms and local isomorphisms are closed under composition, real étale morphisms are closed under composition as well. In fact, we’ve shown morphisms which are locally of finite type have all three properties in the above arguments, so we only need to prove quasicompact, quasiseparated, local isomorphisms have the listed properties.

To prove real étale morphisms are closed under base change, we note that quasiseparated and quasicompact morphisms were shown previously to be closed under base change, and if we can show local isomorphisms are closed under base change, we are done. Fix a pullback square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\times_{Y}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

with
f
real étale, and let
q
be a point in
T\times_{Y}X
. Fix an open neighborhood
U
about
\pi_{X}(q)
on which
f|_{U}
is an isomorphism onto an open
V\subseteq Y
. We make take the fiber product of the above diagram with
V
over
Y
to yield
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(V)\times_{V}f^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

We know
U\subseteq f^{-1}(V)
, and that the restriction of
f
to
U
is an isomorphism. Consequently, in the larger fibered square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times_{V}t^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(V)\times_{V}f^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

the morphism
U\times_{V}t^{-1}(V)\to t^{-1}(V)
is an isomorphism. We have now demonstrated the existence of a neighborhood
U\times_{V}t^{-1}(V)
of
q
and an isomorphism onto its image
t^{-1}(V)
.

Finally, we prove left cancellation holds for real étale morphisms. Suppose
g\circ f
and
g
are real étale morphisms. Since real étale morphisms are quasiseparated, and quasiseparated morphisms have left cancellation,
f
is quasiseparated, and since
g\circ f
is quasicompact as well,
f
is quasiseparated. Finally, to show
f
is a local isomorphism, let
x\in X
, and let
U_{0}
be a neighborhood of
x
on which
(g\circ f)\lvert_{U_{0}}
is an isomorphism onto a neighborhood
U_{2}
of
g(f(x))
. Since
g
is a local isomorphism, select a neighborhood
U_{1}\subseteq g^{-1}(U_{2})
of
f(x)
such that
g\lvert_{U_{1}}
is an isomorphism onto an open
V_{2}\subseteq U_{2}
. We then have an isomorphism

\displaystyle\left(g\lvert_{U_{1}}\right)^{-1}\circ(g\circ f)\biggr\lvert_{(g\circ f)^{-1}(V_{2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(g\lvert_{U_{1}}\right)^{-1}\circ g\lvert_{U_{1}}\circ f\biggr\lvert_{(g\circ f)^{-1}(V_{2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\biggr\lvert_{(g\circ f)^{-1}(V_{2})},

so
f
is real étale and the proposition is proved
∎

Remark 74
.

The following defintion is to be sharply contrasted with Scheiderer’s real surjective families and his construction of the real étale site found in
[
7
]
. While Scheiderer constructs the real étale site over a scheme
X
, he completely avoids defining ’real étale’ in the real closed category. This choice allows Scheiderer to use the full power of known results in the scheme category, but Scheiderer’s approach does not take advantage of how structured real closed spaces are as a category. Additionally, since quasicompact morphisms of real closed spaces have constructible images, we can certainly define site and topos structures on images of real étale morphisms. These subsets and their intersections are the main subsets of real closed spaces we compute with, and there is no need to give such structures to arbitrary subsets as was done in (
[
7
]
,8). I will try to point out other moments of sharp contrast to Scheiderer’s work as they arise.

In order to put a site structure suitable for motivic cohomology on the category of real closed spaces over a fixed real closed
S
, we need to specify a definition of Nisnevich cover. Luckily, since our definition of real étale morphisms are local isomorphisms, real étale morphisms easily lend themselves to defining Nisnevich covers. Also, since real étale morphisms are quasicompact, every cover can be refined to a finite cover. We make the following precise below:

Definition 75
.

A
real étale cover
of a real closed space
X
is a collection of real étale morphims
\{p_{\lambda}:U_{\lambda}\to X\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
such that

\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}p_{\lambda}(U_{\lambda})=X.

Proposition 76
.

Let
\{p_{\lambda}:U_{\lambda}\to X\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a real étale cover of a quasicompact real closed space
X
. Then

i.

we can refine the cover
\{p_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
to be finite;

\{p_{i}:U_{i}\to X\}_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq\{p_{\lambda}:U_{\lambda}\to X\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}

and

ii.

in this finite refinement
\{p_{i}:U_{i}\to X\}_{i=1}^{n}
, for each
x\in X
, there are points
y_{i_{1}}\in U_{i_{1}},\ldots,y_{i_{k}}\in U_{i_{k}}
such that
\rho(x)\to\rho(y_{i_{j}})
is an isormorphism of real closed fields for each
j
.

Proof.

We can prove ii. by noting that for
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
, there is a neighborhood
W_{i_{j}}
of
x
which is mapped to isomorphically by some open
V_{i_{j}}\subseteq U_{i_{j}}
. We may then restrict the codomain of each
p_{i_{j}}
to

\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}W_{i_{j}},

and since
p_{i_{j}}
is a local isomorphism,
y_{i_{j}}=p_{i_{j}}^{-1}(x)
is our desired set of points.

To prove i., we simply note that local isomorphisms are open maps, and so we refine the cover
\{p_{\lambda}(U_{\lambda})\}
to a finite cover
\{p_{i}(U_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. The real closed space morphisms
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
give a finite real étale cover of
X
, concluding this argument.
∎

Remark 77
.

Since each point of
X
is in the image of a local isomorphism of real closed spaces from our real étale cover, it follows that all real étale covers have the Nisnevich property.

If we fix a real closed space
X
, we have a natural candidate for a site structure on
X
given by the real étale covers, and we formalize this in the proposition below;

Proposition 78
.

Let
\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X
be the category with objects as real closed spaces with real étale morphisms to
X
, with morphisms given by commutative triangles over
X
. Then
\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X
together with
\tau
, the real étale covers of
X
, is a site
(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X,\tau)
.

Proof.

Since real étale morphisms are stable under base change and composition, real étale covers are as well, which proves
\tau
satisfies the first three axioms of a Grothendieck pretopology. Since isomorphisms are quasicompact, quasiseparated, local isomorphisms,
\tau
also satisfies the fourth axiom, and
(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X,\tau)
is a site.
∎

Most things we can do with topological spaces also carry over to sites, including sheaves!

Definition 79
.

A functor
\mathscr{F}:(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X)^{\text{op}}\to\underline{Sets}
is a
presheaf
on the site
\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X
, which is a
sheaf
if for any cover
\{p_{\lambda}:U_{\lambda}\to X\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\in\tau
, the sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}(U_{\lambda})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{\kappa,\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}(U_{\kappa}\times_{X}U_{\lambda})}}

is an equalizer sequence.

The sheaves on a Groethendieck site form a topos, and this will be handy for comparing the work of Scheiderer to this text.

3.6.
Relationship between Scheiderer’s
\text{Sh}(Et/X)
and
\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/\rho X)

We will use Lemma 7.29.1 of the stacks project to show these categories of sheaves are equivalent, so we state the lemma in full below

Lemma 80
.

(
[
11
]
, 7.29.1) Let
u:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
be a functor between sites such that

i.

u
is cocontinuous,

ii.

u
is continuous,

iii.

For any morphisms
a,b:U^{\prime}\to U
in
\mathscr{C}
such that
ua=ub
, there is a covering
\{f_{i}:U_{i}^{\prime}\to U^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
such that
a\circ f_{i}=b\circ f_{i}
for all
i
,

iv.

For any morphism
c:u(U^{\prime})\to u(U)
in
\mathscr{D}
, there is a covering
\{f_{i}:U_{i}^{\prime}\to U^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
and morphisms
\{c_{i}:U_{i}^{\prime}\to U\}_{i\in I}
such that
uc_{i}=c\circ uf_{i}
, and

v.

For any object
V
of
\mathscr{D}
, there is a covering family
\{u(U_{i})\to V\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathscr{D}
.

Then the induced morphism

g=(u^{p},u_{p}):\text{Sh}(\mathscr{C})\to\text{Sh}(\mathscr{D})

of topoi is an equivalence of categories.

We will apply this to our sheaf topoi, where our functor
u
is a version of the real closure operation

Definition 81
.

Suppose
X
is a scheme with a real closed point
\text{Spec}(k)\to X
. On an open affine cover
\{\text{Spec}(A_{\ell})\}_{\ell\in L}
of
X
, we have the composition of functors
\sper(\cdot)\circ\Gamma
which sends each open affine to the real closed space
\sper A_{\ell}
. Since the real closure functor commutes with taking quotients and localization by section 1.7, the real spectra
\sper A_{\ell}\cong\sper\rho A_{\ell}
glue to form a real closed space
\rho X
. We thus have a real closure functor

\rho:(Et/X,\text{ret})\to(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/\rho X,\tau)

from the category of etale morphisms over a fixed scheme
X
to the category
\textbf{rc\'{e}}/\rho X
, where
ret
specifies coverings as collections
f_{\lambda}
of étale maps of schemes with codomain
U
such that the collection
\rho f_{\lambda}
surjects onto
\rho U
.

Theorem 82
.

\rho
induces an equivalence of topoi between
\text{Sh}(Et/X)
and
\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/\rho X)
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma 80 to prove the claim, so we will proceed to show all five conditions of the lemma are satisfied.

To prove
\rho
is cocontinuous, let
\{f_{j}:V_{j}\to\rho U\}_{j\in J}
cover
\rho U
for some scheme
U
. Let
\sper B_{i}
be an affine open cover of
\rho U
, and choose
\sper A_{\lambda}\to f_{j}^{-1}(\sper B_{i})
a cover of
f_{j}^{-1}(\sper B_{i})
wherein each
\sper A_{\lambda}
is is chosen small enough that
f_{j}\lvert_{\sper A_{\lambda}}
is an isomorphism and
A_{\lambda}
is real closed. For each triple
(i,j,\lambda)
we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper A_{\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}^{-1}(\sper B_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B_{i}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho U,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}\lvert}

where each horizontal hook arrow is an open immersion. Since
\lambda
depends on
j
, the
rcé
morphisms

\sper A_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow V_{j}\to\rho U

give a refinement of the covering
\{f_{j}:V_{j}\to\rho U\}_{j\in J}
. I further claim that
\{\text{Spec}(A_{\lambda})\to U\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
is a cover of
U
in
ret
. It is slightly easier to show
\{\text{Spec}(A_{\lambda})\to\text{Spec}(B_{i})\}_{(i,\lambda)\in I\times\Lambda}
is a cover, and this implies our claim because any cover of a
ret
cover is a
ret
cover. Cover
f_{j}(\sper A_{\lambda})
with affine opens
\sper(B_{i,b_{1}}),\ldots,\sper(B_{i,b_{m}})
, where
B_{i,b_{k}}
is a localization of both
A_{\lambda}
and
B_{i}
. We have a commutative diagram of rings
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i,b_{k}}}

for each localization
B_{i,b_{k}}
, so by the universal property of the equalizer sequence, we obtain a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{k}}B_{i,b_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{k,l}}B_{i,b_{k}b_{l}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

We extend our open affine covering
\sper(B_{i,b_{k}})
to an open affine covering
\sper(B_{i,b_{k^{\prime}}})
of
\sper B_{i}
, and this new cover yields an equalizer sequence of
B_{i}
-modules
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{k}}B_{i,b_{k^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{k}}B_{i,b_{k^{\prime}}b_{l^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{k}}B_{i,b_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{k}}B_{i,b_{k}b_{l}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

Hence
A_{\lambda}\cong B_{i}
, proving
\text{Spec}(A_{\lambda})\to U
is an étale morphism of schemes.

The next argument will demonstrate
\rho
is a continuous functor. Since tensor products of rings commute with real closures, we only need demonstrate that for any étale cover
\{U_{i}\to U\}
over
X
in the ret topology,
\rho U_{i}\to\rho U
is an
rcé
cover over
\rho X
. For any étale morphism
f:X\to Y
, where
f
is a morphism in a ret cover of
Y
, we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Spec}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Spec}(B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Spec}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

since
f
is locally of finite type, where
\text{Spec}(A)
and
\text{Spec}(B)
are open affines in
X
and
Y
respectively, and
\text{Spec}(A)\to\text{Spec}(B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])
is a closed immersion. We then know

A\cong B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/\langle g_{1}(\underline{x}),\ldots,g_{k}(\underline{x})\rangle,

so in model-theoretic terms, we have the corresponding sentence
\Phi_{\text{Spec}(A)}
in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])
given explicitly by

\exists x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}g_{i}(\underline{x})=0.

\Phi_{\text{Spec}(A)}
defines a constructible subspace
K_{\Phi}
in
\sper B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, and for any homomorphism
\varphi:B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]\to\ell
to a real closed field
\ell
which satisfies
\Phi_{\text{Spec}(A)}
, we know

\varphi(g_{i}(\underline{x}))=0

for all
i\in 1,\ldots,k
. We consequently have the factorization
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

which extends to real closures, so

K_{\Phi}\cong\sper A.

Hence
\rho f
is locally of finite type. By Corollaries 4.5 and 4.6 of
[
8
]
, since being étale can be checked on affines,
\rho f
is quasicompact. By Propositions 4.8, 4.9, and 4.15 in
[
8
]
,

\rho f\lvert_{\sper A}

is separated for all open affines
\sper A
, so
\rho f
is quasiseparated. By (
[
7
]
,5),
\rho f
is a local homeomorphism, and if we can show
\rho f
is a local isomorphism, we will prove
\rho
is continuous.

Since the property ’being étale can be checked on affine opens and étale morphisms are quasicompact, it suffices to analyze étale morphisms
f
in diagrams of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Spec}(k_{1}\times\ldots\times k_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Spec}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Spec}(\kappa(\textbf{p}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Spec}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
\kappa(\textbf{p})
is real closed and
k_{i}/\kappa(\textbf{p})
is a finite separable extension. Since

\kappa(\textbf{p})\to k_{i}\to\overline{\kappa(\textbf{p})}

is a finite extension,
k_{i}
is either real closed or algebraically closed, but since algebraically closed fields have empty real closures, we focus on the real closed case. If
k_{i}
is real closed,
k_{i}\cong\kappa(\textbf{p})
. Consider the local subspace
W_{\sper k_{i}}
of
\sper A
. Since
f
has relative dimension 0 and
\rho f
is surjective by definition of the ret topology, we have an isomorphism of local systems

W_{\sper k_{i}}\cong W_{\sper\kappa(\textbf{p})}

induced by
f
. On a connected open neighborhood
V\subseteq\sper B
about
\sper\kappa(\textbf{p})
, we take a connected connected open
\rho f^{-1}(V)\cap C_{\sper k_{i}}
, where
C_{\sper k_{i}}
is the connected component of
\sper A
containing
\sper k_{i}
. By our isomorphism of local subspaces,

\rho f^{-1}(V)\cap C_{\sper k_{i}}\text{ and }V

are equidimensional. We further shrink the neighborhood
\rho f^{-1}(V)\cap C_{\sper k_{i}}
to
U
so that
\rho f\lvert_{U}
is a homeomorphism onto its image.

To conclude that the local homeomorphism
\rho f
is a local isomorphism, a lemma about local subspaces is required:

Lemma 83
.

The closed points in an
n
-dimensional real closed space
U
have isomorphic local subspaces.

Proof.

Fix a local subspace
W_{x_{1}}
about the closed point
x_{1}=\sper k_{1}
, and fix a top dimensional point
\sper L_{1}\to W_{x_{1}}
so that

L_{1}\cong k_{1}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})

as unordered fields. If
x_{2}=\sper k_{2}
is another closed point of
U
with top dimensional point
\sper L_{2}\to W_{x_{2}}
, we have

\text{tr.deg}L_{2}/k_{2}=\text{tr.deg}L_{1}/k_{1}.

I further claim
\text{tr.deg}(k_{2}/\mathbb{R}_{0})=\text{tr.deg}(k_{1}/\mathbb{R}_{0})
. Towards a contradiction, assume

\text{tr.deg}(k_{2}/\mathbb{R}_{0})\geq\text{tr.deg}(k_{1}/\mathbb{R}_{0}),

so that we a diagram of real spectra of fields
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper L_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper k_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper\mathbb{R}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper L_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sper k_{1}.}

We use the transcendence degree of
k_{2}/k_{1}
to break our analysis of the above diagram into cases. If
\text{tr.deg}(k_{2}/k_{1})=0
,
k_{1}\cong k_{2}
and
\text{tr.deg}(k_{i}/\mathbb{R}_{0})
is a constant function of
i
. Otherwise, we have the equations

\displaystyle\text{tr.deg}(k_{2}/\mathbb{R}_{0})=

\displaystyle\text{tr.deg}(k_{2}/k_{1})+\text{tr.deg}(k_{1}/\mathbb{R}_{0})

\displaystyle\text{tr.deg}(L_{i}/\mathbb{R}_{0})=

\displaystyle\text{tr.deg}(L_{i}/k_{i})+\text{tr.deg}(k_{i}/\mathbb{R}_{0}),

so if
\text{tr.deg}(k_{i}/\mathbb{R}_{0})>0
, we have a finite extension

k_{2}\to k_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}_{0}}k_{2}\to L_{2},

and since

\text{tr.deg}(k_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}_{0}}k_{2}/k_{1})=\text{tr.deg}(k_{1}/\mathbb{R}_{0}),

both are finite. Properties of the transcendence degree of a field then allow us to conclude we’ve reached a contradiction, so
\text{tr.deg}(k_{2}/\mathbb{R}_{0})=\text{tr.deg}(k_{1}/\mathbb{R}_{0})
as originally claimed.
∎

Finally, we know homeomorphisms are closed maps, so for any other local system
W_{x_{0}}
of a closed point
x_{0}
in
U
,

W_{\rho f(x_{1})}\cong W_{x_{1}}\cong W_{x_{0}}\cong W_{\rho f(x_{0})}.

Since being a local isomorphism can be checked on local subspaces, and all local subspaces of closed points have been demonstrated to be isomorphic,
\rho f\lvert_{U}
is an isomorphism onto its image.

To prove
\rho
has property iii., we note that morphisms of real closed spaces in the image of
\rho
are also scheme morphisms, so property iii. holds for any ret cover.

Proving
\rho
has property iv. is very similar to proving
\rho
is cocontinuous! For
\sper B_{\lambda}
an affine open cover of
\rho U
, and
\sper A_{\kappa}
an open affine cover of
\rho U^{\prime}\times_{\rho U}\sper B_{\lambda}
, the desired cover is
\text{Spec}(A_{\kappa})\to U^{\prime}
and the desired collection of morphisms is
\text{Spec}(A_{\kappa})\to U
.

To prove the final property, we note that a real closed space
Y
(over
\rho X
) has a
rcé
-cover
\rho U_{i}
in which each
U_{i}
is étale over
X
. We take an open affine cover of
Y
by real spectra
\sper B_{\lambda}
, and this cover satisfies condition v. This concludes the proof.
∎

3.7.
Simplicial Sheaves

We need to enlarge the category so that real closed spaces and simplices are objects in the same category, in order to construct our own homotopy theory.

We make use of Nisnevich squares in forming homotopy limits and colimits, so we take a deep look at those squares below;

Definition 84
.

Let
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times_{X}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

be a commutative pullback square. This square is an
elementary distinguished Nisnevich square
if
U\to X
is an open immersion,
p
is real étale, and
p^{-1}(X\setminus U)\to X\setminus U
is an isomorphism.

Example 85
.

Let
X=\sper\mathbb{R}[u]
. Let
U
be given by the sentence

\exists u:u<1

in the language
\mathscr{L}_{\leq}(\mathbb{R}[u])
, and let
V
be given by

\exists u:u>0.

I claim
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times_{X}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

is an elementary distinguished Nisnevich square. We know
U
is an open subspace of
X
and
V\hookrightarrow X
is real étale. To analyze the complement of
U
, we consider the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\setminus U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}

The morphisms from both
X\setminus U
and
V
to
X
are inclusions, so the limit over the above diagram is an intersection, and since
X\setminus U\subset V
,

p^{-1}(X\setminus U)\cong X\setminus U

and we have demonstrated what we set out to show.

Remark 86
.

We can generalize the previous example; if
p:V\to X
is a real étale morphism whose image contains
X\setminus U
, we can form a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times_{X}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Since
p
is quasicompact,
p(V)
has its own real closed space structure and is locally constructible in
X
. When we form the fiber product
p^{-1}(X\setminus U)
, we may form it in two stages;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times_{X}(X\setminus U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\setminus U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\setminus U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X\setminus U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where

V\times_{X}(X\setminus U)\cong p^{-1}(X\setminus U).

The previous square is an elementary distinuished Nisnevich square if
p_{X\setminus U}
is an isomorphism.
For example, if
X=\sper(\mathbb{R}[x])\setminus\{-1,1\}
, let
U=X\setminus\{0\}
, and let
V\subset\sper\mathbb{R}[x,y]
be given by

\begin{cases}x=(y-1)^{2}&\text{ if }x>1\\
y=0&\text{ if }-1<x<1\\
x=-(y+1)^{2}&\text{ if }x<-1.\end{cases}

The projection to the
x
-coordinate is a real étale morphism
V\to X
, and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

is an elementary distinguished Nisnevich square.

Now we form the simplicial model category central to our study, which is a category large enough to contain an
n
-simplex for each
n
, as well as every real closed space over
X
;

Definition 87
.

Let
\Delta
be the category whose objects are finite totally ordered sets
[n]=(\{0,1,2,\ldots,n\},\leq)
, with order-preserving functions as our morphisms. Let
\text{PreSh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X)
be the category of presheaves on the real étale site of
X
. A
simplicial presheaf on

X
is a functor
\mathscr{F}:\Delta^{\text{op}}\to\text{PreSh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X)
. A simplicial presheaf is a
simplicial sheaf
if, for each
[n]
and real étale cover
\{U_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
, the sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}_{n}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}_{n}(U_{\lambda})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{\kappa,\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}_{n}(U_{\kappa}\times_{X}U_{\lambda})}}

is an equalizer. We denote the collection of these sheaves
\mathscr{F}
by
\Delta^{op}\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X)
.

Example 88
.

Fix a real closed space
Y
. Consider the functor
\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X}(-,Y)
from
\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X
to
\underline{Sets}
, and define

\mathscr{F}_{n}:=\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X}(-,Y).

I claim
\mathscr{F}
defines a simplicial sheaf on
X
, where each morphism in
\Delta
is sent to the identity natural transformation
\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X}(-,Y)\to\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X}(-,Y)
. Since real closed spaces form a locally small category,
\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X}(-,Y)
is a set when evaluated on any real closed space
V
, so
\mathscr{F}
is a simplicial presheaf. To prove
\mathscr{F}
is a simplicial sheaf, let
\{U_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
be a real étale cover of
X
, and let
n
be nonnegative. Given a section

(\sigma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\in{\displaystyle\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}_{n}(U_{\lambda})}

such that for each pair
\lambda,\kappa\in\Lambda
,

\sigma_{\lambda}\biggr\lvert_{U_{\kappa}\times_{X}U_{\lambda}}=\sigma_{\kappa}\biggr\lvert_{U_{\kappa}\times_{X}U_{\lambda}}.

We can give a function
\sigma\in\mathscr{F}_{n}(X)=\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X}(X,Y)
which restricts to
\sigma_{\lambda}
on
U_{\lambda}
, proving sections of
\mathscr{F}_{n}
glue. To show that this gluing is unique, we map the singleton ’test set’
\{t\}
to our diagram;
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}_{n}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}_{n}(U_{\lambda})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{\kappa,\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}_{n}(U_{\kappa}\times_{X}U_{\lambda})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{t\}.}

Glueing functions of sets gives a factorization
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{F}_{n}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}_{n}(U_{\lambda})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\displaystyle\prod_{\kappa,\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathscr{F}_{n}(U_{\kappa}\times_{X}U_{\lambda})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{t\},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists}

and this factorization is unique since functions
\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X}(X,Y)
are well-defined by their restrictions to the opens in our real étale cover.

For any morphism
f:X\to Y
of real closed spaces, there is a functor

f_{\ast}:\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X)\to\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/Y),

which takes a sheaf
\mathscr{F}
on
X
and outputs a sheaf
f_{\ast}\mathscr{F}
on
Y
. On a covering open
V\to Y
of
Y
,

f_{\ast}\mathscr{F}(V):=\mathscr{F}(f^{-1}(V)),

where
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a fibered square. Consequently, we have a morphism

\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/Y}(-,Y)\to f_{\ast}\text{Hom}_{\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X}(-,X)

of sheaves, given by taking the real closed étale map
V\to Y
to the real closed étale map
f^{-1}(V)\to X
.

Proposition 89
.

A morphism
f:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
in
\Delta^{\text{op}}\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/X)
is a monomorphism if and only if, for each
n\geq 0
and each
rcé
-morphism
U\to X
, the set map

f_{n,U}:\mathscr{X}_{n}(U)\to\mathscr{Y}_{n}(U)

is a monomorphism.

Proof.

Let
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

be a commutative diagram, i.e.
f\circ g^{1}=f\circ g^{2}
. If
f
is a monomorphism,
g^{1}=g^{2}
by definition, so

g_{n,U}^{1}=g_{n,U}^{2}

for all
U
and
n
. We have shown
f_{n,U}
is a monomorphism for all choices of
n
and
U
.

On the other hand, suppose
f_{n,U}
is a monomorphism for any choice of
n
and
U
. We then know
g_{n,U}^{1}=g_{n,U}^{2}
for all
n
and
U
. On a
rcé
cover
\{U_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
of
X
,
g^{i}
is a sheaf morphism, so for
\beta,\lambda\in\Lambda

g_{n,U_{\lambda}}^{1}\lvert_{U_{\lambda}\times_{X}U_{\beta}}=g_{n,U_{\beta}}^{1}\lvert_{U_{\lambda}\times_{X}U_{\beta}}.

Hence the sections
g_{n,U_{\lambda}}^{i}
glue to form a sheaf morphism
g_{n}^{i}
, and since these morphisms are equal on each open
U_{\lambda}
,

g_{n}^{1}=g_{n}^{2}

for all
n
, proving our claim.
∎

3.8.
The Simplicial Model Category Structure

Definition 90
.

A morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
of simplicial sheaves on the site
(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/T,\tau)
is a
weak equivalence
if for any point
x:\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/T)\to\underline{Sets}
of
(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/T,\tau)
,
x(f)
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets (i.e. a homotopy equivalence on geometric realizations).

Theorem 91
.

We take the class of monomorphisms in
\Delta^{\text{op}}\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/T)
to be cofibrations. The class of morphisms which have the right lifting property with respect to any cofibration which is also a weak equivalence are called fibrations. The classes
(\textbf{W},\textbf{C},\textbf{F})
give the category
\Delta^{\text{op}}\text{Sh}(\textbf{rc\'{e}}/T)
the structure of a model category

Proof.

This result follows directly from
[
4
]
, since they prove these classes of morphisms give a simplicial model structure to the simplicial sheaves on
any
Grothendieck site.

To give a bit more detail, proving these three classes of morphisms satisfy all the axioms of a model category
except
that each morphism
f:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
has a factorization into a trivial cofibration followed by a fibration, is straightforward. To prove the 5th axiom of a model category, Jardine uses two techniques which are too complicated to fully explain here; Boolean Localization and The Bounded Cofibration Condition. Boolean localization is used to show that we can check whether a morphism is a local weak equivalence on a Boolean Algebra instead of the given site. The Bounded Cofibration Condition is a theorem, which says as long as our site
\textbf{rc\'{e}}/T
doesn’t have too many morphisms, morphisms from a ’small’ object can be factored into a monomorphism and a trivial cofibration.
∎

We may Bousfield localize this model structure with respect to the class of morphisms

\{f:[0,1]_{k}\times_{T}X\to X\}_{k=\rho k,X\to T\text{ is }\textbf{rc\'{e}}}

to form the homotopy category of simplicial sheaves.

4.
The Future: Understanding Representable Cohomology on RCS

I suspect we can further contextualize the result of
[
3
]
in the homotopy category formed above that there is no de Rham cohomology for semialgebraic spaces using the motivic homotopy theory and resulting representable cohomology theories developed here. It is unclear whether or not the theory of motives is necessary to contextualize Professor Huber’s work, but I hope to investigate further in the future. I also suspect there is a version of gradient descent, using ’local subspaces like tangent bundles’, which can be done in real closed spaces, especially if those real closed spaces are semialgebraic.
